Since the destination (`.hephaestus/`) is supposed to stay git-excluded, the first fix would have had every doc-review run silently re-track exactly the files the `docs/` → `.hephaestus/` migration was trying to stop tracking — defeating its own purpose.

**Proper fix.** For a tracked source moving into a gitignored destination, the correct sequence is copy-then-`git rm`, not `git mv`: copy the file to the destination (left untracked, respecting `.gitignore` naturally since it's a plain filesystem write, not a git operation), then `git rm <source>` to stage only the deletion. For a source that was never committed, `git rm` fails with "did not match any files" — the fallback there is a plain `rm`, since there's nothing to stage. `7b94f4a` applied this same corrected sequence to both the docs-sweep instruction and a second, identically-flawed root-level stray-file cleanup instruction in the same YAML file.

**Commits:** `2a5f749`, `7b94f4a`.

**Doc comparison.** Not covered above, and not a Python code fix at all — both fix commits are entirely `config/workflows/autopilot/doc_review.yaml` prompt-text changes (the "code" here is an agent instruction, not a function). Worth flagging as a standalone, non-obvious git-behavior fact regardless of file scope: `git mv` re-tracking a gitignored destination is exactly the kind of thing a future contributor writing a *new* sweep/archival step (Python or prompt) would plausibly get wrong the same way twice in the same afternoon, and there's no test or lint that would catch the mistake before it silently re-tracks output next to `.hephaestus/`.

## Bug-Fix History: Task-Creation-Claim, Races & Deduplication Patterns

*Derived from a fresh read of the actual commit diffs behind 54 `fix:` commits in this theme, independent of the file-structure analysis above.*

### 1. The task-creation-claim primitive: claim/release/staleness triad

**Problem.** `PhaseExecution.task_creation_claimed_at` is the mutex for "who gets to create this phase's next task." Getting the triad right is deceptively hard because each of its three moving parts fails independently and silently: a bad *claim* lets two callers both win, a bad *release* wedges the claim forever, and a bad *staleness comparison* makes the self-heal that's supposed to catch an abandoned claim never fire.

**Proper fix, by failure mode, evidenced across four commits over three weeks:**
- **Staleness comparison must use the same clock as the writer.** `65294a0` (2026-07-21) found `_claim_phase_task_creation` stamping with `datetime.now()` (ambient local time) while `_release_stale_task_creation_claims` compared against a second, independent `datetime.now()` call. Two processes/threads only agree on "now" if they share a TZ; when they didn't, a claim sat ~2 hours old, provably past the 8-minute timeout, but its staleness sweep computed a "now" hours behind the claim's timestamp and never released it. Fix: both call sites moved to `datetime.utcnow()`. This is the exact incident the project's `utc-only` CLAUDE.md invariant was written to generalize from — good confirmation the invariant is not theoretical.
- **Release must anchor to the guarded row's own timestamp, not "now" at release time.** `75e1e52` (2026-08-14): `_release_phase_task_creation_claim` stamped `execution.started_at = datetime.utcnow()`, but release always runs *after* the guarded task is already committed by a caller that can spend real seconds on enrichment/embedding/dedup/queue checks first — so "now" was reliably later than the task's own `created_at`. Downstream, `_case_in_progress_complete` filters `Task.created_at >= execution.started_at` to find in-cycle tasks; the too-late `started_at` excluded the very task the claim was guarding, made the phase look task-less, and self-heal spawned a duplicate ~15s later. Fix: `started_at = earliest_task.created_at` (queried fresh), never `utcnow()`. General lesson: never stamp a "when did this happen" field with the current wall clock when the event you're describing already happened earlier and you can look its real timestamp up.
- **Claim the phase you're about to write to, not the phase you evaluated.** `45dd5a2` and `85fc3c5` (both 2026-08-14): `_create_phase_task`'s only protection was an existing-task check, but two call sites (`_fire_phase_transition`, `_resolve_arbitration_outcome`, and separately `_create_corrective_task`) claim their *source* phase and then dispatch to a *different target* phase (a goto/arbitration destination) with no claim on it at all. A slow dispatch (worktree setup + tmux launch) crossing the orphan cutoff let a second caller decide the in-flight task was dead and spawn a sibling — two goto tasks landed on `architecture_design` 85s apart, observed live. Fix: `target_already_claimed: bool = False` parameter — when the caller hasn't already claimed the exact `phase_id` it's about to write to, the function claims it internally (with the same 8-minute staleness fallback inlined), and releases only the claim it itself took (`own_claim` flag), via a raw `UPDATE` rather than the shared release helper, since a bail-out path shouldn't flip `PhaseExecution.status`.
- **A third, independent evaluator needs the same claim as the sweep — but a different release.** `fe9a141` (2026-08-14): `fire_spec_gate_if_ready` in `task_completion_service.py` is a synchronous, request-triggered phase-completion evaluator that runs `build_phase_output` (can run pytest for minutes) and `mark_phase_complete`'s `evaluate()` (an LLM call) — and took no claim at all, so a concurrent sweep tick could re-evaluate the same phase completion mid-flight. Fix takes the shared `_claim_phase_task_creation(session, phase.id)` before the slow work, but releases with a raw `UPDATE ... SET task_creation_claimed_at = None` in a `finally`, deliberately bypassing `_release_phase_task_creation_claim` — that helper also flips `PhaseExecution.status`, which would corrupt whatever state `mark_phase_complete` already left the row in.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** fully confirmed, not new. The Task & Dispatch Services section above already names all four commits and describes the same mechanism; the Refactor Plan's §4.1 targets exactly this consolidation. What reading the actual diffs adds is precision on *why* each fix is shaped the way it is (e.g., the raw-`UPDATE`-not-shared-helper detail in `fe9a141`, and the `own_claim`/`target_already_claimed` split in `45dd5a2`) — useful as implementation detail for whoever builds the "one reusable primitive" the docs recommend.

### 2. Sibling-task dedup queries and the NULL-match trap

**Problem.** Several independent "does this phase already have an active sibling task?" checks exist (`_retry_failed_tasks`'s pre-check and post-dispatch-failure fallback, `create_agent_for_task_direct`'s phase-sibling guard, `task_similarity_service.py`). All of them filter `Task.phase_id == phase_id`. In SQLAlchemy this compiles to `IS NULL` when `phase_id` is Python `None` — a phase-less task (one created ad hoc, e.g. an adversarial-re-review request outside the phase pipeline) then matches *every other phase-less task in the entire database*, not "no sibling."

**Proper fix:**
- `54d1814` (2026-08-14): wraps both `_retry_failed_tasks` sibling queries in `if phase_id:` before running them, defaulting to "no sibling" when falsy — mirroring a guard that already existed one call up in `create_agent_for_task_direct` (`if task.phase_id:`) but hadn't been copied to these two newer, independently-added query sites. Observed live: task `5b29d427` (phase-less) was wrongly marked `duplicated` of task `a7430ccc`, an unrelated task from five weeks earlier — the only thing they had in common was both being NULL.
- `01558db` (2026-08-13, one day earlier): a related but distinct bug in the same function — `create_agent_for_task_direct` returns `None` both for a genuine creation failure *and* for its own deliberate "another active task already owns this phase" guard declining to dispatch. `_retry_failed_tasks` treated both identically, raising and burning a retry budget on a decision that was actually correct (a manually-reset task collided with a legitimately-created fresh one for the same phase). Fix: check for an active sibling *before* treating a `None` return as failure; if one exists, mark `"duplicated"` with `duplicate_of_task_id` set (not `"failed"` — and notably, the first attempt used `status="skipped"`, which isn't a valid value under the DB's `Task.status` CHECK constraint and silently produced a different, equally confusing failure, caught by the regression test).
- `f797a94` (2026-08-10): the same "active sibling on this phase" check gets bolted on a third time, as the last line of defense inside `create_agent_for_task_direct` itself, specifically to close the race between `_retry_failed_tasks` (task-level, no claim) and `_advance_phases` (phase-level, claim-protected) deciding to dispatch in adjacent sweep ticks — two development agents were observed invoked simultaneously for the same workflow.

**Relation to file-structure analysis — sharper synthesis available here.** The analysis above separately flags `task_similarity_service.py:71`'s `query.filter(Task.phase_id is None)` (Python `is`, not SQLAlchemy `.is_(None)`, always compiles to hard-`False`) as a *still-open* bug. Read together with `54d1814`'s *fixed* `== None → IS NULL` bug, these are the same defect family in mirror image: one query treats NULL phase_id as matching everything, the other treats it as matching nothing — both wrong, both live in the same "is this task a duplicate of another" problem space, and neither one's fix informed the other. That's a concrete argument for centralizing sibling-lookup as one helper with the NULL-guard baked in once, rather than trusting each of the ≥4 call sites to remember it independently — the "reimplemented per call site" framing understates it slightly: it's not just the claim triad that's copy-pasted, this NULL-handling gotcha is too.

### 3. Duplicate agent dispatch: an unnamed second guard family outside the claim primitive

**Problem.** Distinct from the `task_creation_claimed_at` mutex, a second, never-consolidated family of ad hoc duplicate-dispatch guards lives in `src/monitoring/monitor.py` and `src/agents/manager.py`, addressing the same "two agents get spawned for one phase" symptom but via different mechanisms entirely — none of them touch `PhaseExecution.task_creation_claimed_at`.

**Proper fix, by mechanism:**
- **In-memory per-phase cooldown.** `9a2fd48` (2026-06-25): when a task completes, its agent's cleanup is asynchronous — the agent row stays `"working"` and the tmux session stays alive briefly. A monitor pass firing in that window sees no *active task* (status-only check) and spawns a second agent. Fix adds two layers: (1) an active-agent join query (`Agent.status IN (working/idle/starting)` joined to `Task.phase_id`) that checks agent liveness directly instead of inferring it from task status, and (2) a 30-second in-memory `_phase_last_created[phase_id]` cooldown dict as a backstop for same-cycle double-fire before the first commit is even visible to the second path.
- **Status-transition idempotency check.** `7dc2e0d` (2026-06-25, same day): the existing active-task guard missed a case where the advance logic double-fired minutes apart, by which point the prior task was already `"done"` (not active) and slipped the guard. Fix: on `action == "continue"`, if the execution is already `"in_progress"` *and* the phase already has a `"done"` task, skip — goto/retry re-enter from `completed`/`pending` with a different action value, so legitimate re-runs are unaffected.
- **Agent-row-level guard, one layer below all of the above.** `9801da7` (2026-07-21): `AgentManager.create_agent_for_task` itself gained a check for an existing active agent on the *same task* before creating a new one — closing the case where two independent callers (orchestrator + `task_completion_service`) both try to spawn for the same task, which none of the phase-level guards above are positioned to catch.

**Relation to file-structure analysis: new, not previously named.** Neither the analysis nor plan doc frames this as its own cluster — the "N-th independent implementation" cross-cutting theme is broad enough to cover it in principle, but its concrete task-creation-claim writeup is scoped to the five `task_creation_claimed_at` call sites in `orchestrator.py`/`task_completion_service.py` and doesn't mention `monitor.py`'s cooldown dict, `manager.py`'s active-agent guard, or `create_agent_for_task_direct`'s phase-sibling check (`f797a94`, covered in §2 above) as siblings of that same problem. They're a second, structurally different duplicate-dispatch defense layered on top of — not built from — the claim primitive, and worth naming explicitly in any refactor: consolidating the claim triad alone would not remove these four call sites' independent, still-necessary guards.

### 4. Sweep vs. synchronous-path races: two schedulers deciding the same thing

**Problem.** Autopilot has (at least) two independent drivers of phase advancement for the same workflow: `run_single_workflow`'s inline polling loop (the primary path while a workflow is actively being watched) and the background `_advance_phases` sweep (a fallback for workflows that lost their poll loop, e.g. across a backend restart). Both call the same phase-transition logic; nothing originally stopped both from firing on the same workflow in the same window.

**Proper fix, showing an escalation from ad hoc to structural:**
- `fa59638` (2026-07-07, earliest): `_case_start_first_phase`'s existing-task count check could run before `/start_workflow_execution`'s synchronous initial-task-creation commit was visible to it — a narrow window, closed with a **check → `time.sleep(0.5)` → recheck** pattern (explicitly likened in the commit message to "the same mitigation style as the earlier agent-auth cross-thread race fix"). This is the check-then-sleep-then-recheck anti-pattern the claim primitive elsewhere replaces with an atomic UPDATE — at this point in the codebase's history (pre-claim-consolidation for this call site), it was the available mitigation, not a chosen design.
- `ce66b4d` (2026-06-29): bundles three unrelated fixes in one commit, one of which is an early, narrower version of the same idempotency idea the claim primitive would later formalize — `mark_phase_complete` gained `if execution.status == "completed": skip` to stop the monitor and the spec-gate from both evaluating the same phase completion. This is the direct ancestor of the "excludes own execution" self-exclusion fixes in sub-pattern 5 below; at this point it only guards re-*evaluation*, not the downstream reset query that would later reopen the same hole.
- `e5fd9d8` (2026-08-10): the structural fix. Introduces a `threading.Lock`-guarded `_actively_monitored_workflows` set with `_register_monitored_workflow`/`_unregister_monitored_workflow`/`_is_workflow_monitored` accessors — the background sweep now explicitly skips any workflow currently in that set, making "who evaluates this workflow" an actual mutual-exclusion decision rather than a hope that timing rarely overlaps. Paired fix in the same commit: the inline call previously only refreshed `pending`/`in_progress` counts after firing a transition, leaving `active_agents`/`done`/`failed`/`non_terminal` stale for the rest of that poll iteration — now refreshes all counts.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the sweep-vs-inline-path duplication is implicit in the analysis's description of the sweep as "the fallback for workflows that lost their poll loop," but it doesn't call out `_actively_monitored_workflows` as its own concurrency primitive worth documenting, nor trace `fa59638 → ce66b4d → e5fd9d8` as an evolution from sleep-based mitigation to lock-based exclusion. Worth adding: it's a second, non-claim-based concurrency mechanism (a mutual-exclusion *set*, not a DB mutex) that the "extract task-creation-claim into one primitive" recommendation doesn't cover, because it isn't guarding task creation — it's guarding which scheduler is allowed to evaluate a given workflow at all.

### 5. GOTO/rewind stale-`PhaseExecution` reset: four commits widening the same query

**Problem.** When the pipeline fires a `goto` (rewinding to an earlier phase, e.g. QA sending execution back to development), every `PhaseExecution` row between/at the rewind target needs resetting so a later re-entry re-runs cleanly instead of finding stale `in_progress`/`completed` state. The query that decides *which* rows to reset was independently narrow, then widened, then found to catch its own caller's row, across four commits and three separate files over seven weeks — a clean illustration of the "N-th independent implementation" theme playing out step by step rather than as a single historical fact.

**Proper fix, tracked through its own evolution:**
- `6d66e0d` (2026-06-26): first version. On goto, reset `PhaseExecution` rows with `Phase.order >= target.order AND Phase.order < phase.order AND status == "in_progress"` to `"completed"`. Also separately guards `monitor.py`'s `_check_phase_progression` from racing ahead to a pending successor while any phase is still `in_progress`.
- `e15042f` (2026-07-15): found this too narrow on a live incident ("no adversarial_review_result.json found"). The reset only covered rows *between* target and current, and only `in_progress` — it missed `completed` rows *at or after* the current phase (e.g. `adversarial_review`, which sits after `architectural_review` in phase order but is itself the goto target's later re-entry point). A stale `"completed"` `adversarial_review` execution meant the sweep re-evaluated it without re-running it, finding artifacts already consumed by the first goto, and sent development back with a nonsensical rejection reason. Fix: widen the filter to `Phase.order >= target.order` (drop the upper bound entirely) and `status.in_(["in_progress", "completed"])`, and reset to `"pending"` (not `"completed"`) with `task_creation_claimed_at = None` so re-entry gets a genuinely fresh cycle. Applied identically to both `_handle_evaluation_goto` and `_handle_force_goto` (arbitration path) — already two independent copies of the same query at this point.
- `084edcf` (2026-08-14): the widened query immediately created a self-inflicted bug — the firing phase's own order is always `>= target.order` by construction, so the reset query caught the phase's *own just-closed execution*, flipping its `"completed"` mark straight back to `"pending"` one line after setting it, defeating `mark_phase_complete`'s idempotency guard. A later sweep tick then re-evaluated the same stale gate result and fired an identical goto again, racing the first — observed live as `design_review`'s goto decision firing twice, 84s apart, off one unchanged `challenge.md`. Fix: add `PhaseExecution.id != execution.id` to both handlers' copies of the query.
- `fe9a141` (2026-08-14, same day): the *identical* self-exclusion bug, found a third time, in a *third* independent copy of this query — `task_completion_service.py`'s `fire_spec_gate_if_ready`, which has its own inline reset-stale-executions block that never inherited `084edcf`'s fix because it isn't the same code, just the same logic re-typed.

**Relation to file-structure analysis — this is the sharpest new synthesis in this theme.** The Refactor Observations above note the duplication exists ("`084edcf`'s self-exclusion fix had to be applied to both" `_handle_evaluation_goto`/`_handle_force_goto`) but frame it as a single fact about the current code, not as a traceable four-commit arc. The diffs show something the static-code read couldn't: this wasn't "duplicated code, fixed once, forgot the other copy" — it was *re-derived* three separate times (`6d66e0d`, then widened in `e15042f`, then self-exclusion added in `084edcf`, then the same self-exclusion gap rediscovered independently in a third file the same day in `fe9a141`), each round only as good as whichever engineer/session happened to be looking at that one call site. That's a materially stronger case for the "one shared reset-stale-executions-on-goto helper" recommendation than "duplicated logic" alone — the same *bug*, not just the same *code shape*, recurred three times because the fix lived in the copy, not in a shared function.

### 6. Stale cross-entity status sync: denormalized status fields drifting apart

**Problem.** Several entities carry their own denormalized "is this thing still active" status that's supposed to track a related entity's real state: `Workflow.status` should reflect whether its `Feature` is done; `Task` rows should reflect whether their assigned `Agent` is still alive; a background sweep's scope filter (`wf_status == "active"`) should cover every workflow that can actually have stale rows. Each of these pairings drifted independently, and each fix is structurally the same move: stop trusting the field that's supposed to be updated and instead check the authoritative downstream state directly.

**Proper fix:**
- `7edd666` (2026-08-04): `get_project_design_status`'s workflow-matching is a broad `LIKE` on the bare design filename (since a design is re-run once per decomposed feature, several workflows share that filename substring). The endpoint's "any matching workflow active wins" rule trusted each workflow's own `Workflow.status`, which was never cleaned up after `Feature.status` reached `"completed"` for one sibling feature — making the *whole design* look permanently active in the UI. Fix: exclude a workflow from the "any active" check whenever its linked `Feature.status` is already `completed`/`skipped`, i.e. trust the more-authoritative downstream field over the denormalized one.
- `c3622c9` (2026-07-24): `_sync_stale_feature_statuses`'s original case only repaired a `Feature` whose *linked* `Workflow` had completed. It missed features with `workflow_id IS NULL` entirely (never linked, e.g. skipped or a linking bug). Fix adds a second case: an unlinked feature whose design siblings include a completed one and no active one is inferred `"completed"` too — staleness detection widened to a category of row the original query structurally couldn't see, not just a timing-window fix.
- `90c2a73` (2026-08-10): `_clean_stale_assigned_tasks` (tasks still `assigned`/`in_progress` pointing at a `terminated` agent) only ran inside the sweep's `wf_status == "active"` branch — so a workflow that finished (or was force-completed) with an orphaned task never got that task cleaned, ever. Fix: move the cleanup call outside the active-only guard so it runs for every workflow status; retry/arbitration logic correctly stays active-only since those only make sense for a live workflow.
- `4d8e47d` (2026-06-29): the underlying cleanup didn't exist yet at all — `attempt_recovery` had no step that noticed a task was still `"assigned"`/`"in_progress"` to an agent whose own `status == "terminated"`. Such tasks hung forever with no path back to retry. Fix adds the direct join-and-check: for every non-terminal task with an `assigned_agent_id`, if that agent is terminated, flip the task to `"failed"` so the next recovery cycle can retry it — the CLAUDE.md agent-termination invariant (`current_task_id` cleared on termination) is about the *agent* row; this is the mirror-image gap on the *task* side that invariant doesn't cover by itself.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** not discussed as a named cluster in either document (both focus on the `Workflow.status`/`AutopilotProject.is_active` invariant and the task-creation-claim primitive specifically). This is a genuinely separate staleness-detection theme worth naming on its own: multiple pairs of denormalized status fields (`Workflow.status`/`Feature.status`, `Task.status`/`Agent.status`) each need their own reconciliation sweep, each sweep's scope filter needs auditing independently (a sweep gated on the wrong entity's status silently excludes an entire category of stale rows), and each fix in this group is a scope-widening, not a locking fix — a different failure mode from sub-patterns 1–5's race conditions, but the same "staleness" symptom family.

### 7. Transcript-redraw deduplication (adjacent domain, not a race/claim bug)

**Problem.** A cluster of four commits in three days (`2306dcd`, `f91d600`, `063c416`, plus `5241756`) all fix duplicate *lines* appearing in agent transcript output — but this is a text-processing problem, not a database race: when a CLI agent's tmux pane is captured without carriage-return (`\r`) sequences preserved (old `pipe-pane` mode), a progress redraw (spinner, streaming partial text) that would normally overwrite a line in place instead appears as two separate, near-duplicate lines in the captured log.

**Proper fix, and why it kept recurring:**
- `2306dcd` (2026-07-09): baseline heuristic — a line is a partial redraw of the next if the next line, ANSI-stripped, starts with the current line's (also stripped) text and is longer. Drop the shorter one.
- `f91d600` (2026-07-10, next day): the prefix heuristic missed redraws where the *tool name* stayed the same but the *argument* changed (e.g. `read /Users/hmuh` → `read ~/code/.../config.py`) — not a literal string prefix. Added a second heuristic: same leading tool-name token (`read|write|edit|bash|subagent|mcp`), shorter line is the partial redraw.
- `063c416` (2026-07-10, same day): the prefix heuristic also missed redraws separated by blank lines (read/write output with blank-line padding between the partial and final draw). Fix looks ahead past empty lines when searching for a prefix match, plus collapses consecutive empty lines left behind by the removals.
- `5241756` (2026-07-09): a related fix in the same window filtering TUI chrome messages and limiting how aggressively dedup runs (to avoid over-collapsing legitimately repeated content).

**Relation to file-structure analysis: out of scope for this theme, included for completeness.** Neither document discusses transcript rendering at all — this is UI/agent-output-plumbing, not orchestrator concurrency, and doesn't touch the task-creation-claim primitive or any DB row. It's worth flagging as its own smaller lesson distinct from sub-patterns 1–6: heuristic, string-similarity-based deduplication (as opposed to the DB-mutex-based dedup elsewhere in this theme) is inherently patch-prone — each fix closed one specific redraw shape (literal prefix, same-tool-different-arg, blank-line-separated) without a structural reason to believe the next shape wouldn't need its own patch, which is exactly what happened three times in two days. Not a candidate for the "extract one primitive" treatment the DB-side patterns get, since there's no single correct algorithm being reinvented — just an unbounded set of terminal-redraw shapes being discovered one at a time.

---

**Overall assessment of this theme.** The task-creation-claim primitive (sub-pattern 1) is confirmed, not revised, by this pass — the file-structure analysis's read of the current code matches what the historical diffs show it took to get there. The higher-value finding from reading actual diffs rather than current code is sub-pattern 5: the GOTO stale-execution-reset bug is not one duplicated block that occasionally goes stale, it's a bug that was *independently rediscovered* three times in three files, which is a stronger and more specific argument for consolidation than "this code is copy-pasted." Sub-patterns 3 and 6 name two clusters (the non-claim duplicate-dispatch guards, and the denormalized cross-entity status sync sweeps) that neither existing document calls out as their own thing, both real and both distinct from the claim primitive itself — worth folding into whatever consolidation plan follows, since fixing `task_creation_claimed_at` alone would leave both untouched.

## Bug-Fix History: Agent Termination & Restart Patterns

*Derived from a fresh read of the actual commit diffs behind 40 `fix:` commits in this theme, independent of the file-structure analysis above.*

### 1. Dangling tasks left pointing at a terminated agent — the core invariant, fixed independently at least five times

**The recurring problem.** `Agent.status = "terminated"` is one write; "reset the `Task` that agent was working on back to `pending`" is a *second*, logically separate write that many call sites simply forgot. A task left `in_progress`/`assigned` pointing at a dead agent is invisible to every dispatch path (only `pending` tasks get picked up), so it sits stranded until an unrelated periodic sweep (`attempt_recovery`'s stale-assigned-task cleanup) eventually notices the mismatch and fails it with a generic "terminated unexpectedly" reason — discarding any real, uncommitted work the agent had already produced.

**Why it kept recurring:** there was never one termination primitive. `AgentManager.terminate_agent` in `src/agents/manager.py`, a second parallel primitive `terminate_agent_direct` in `src/autopilot/orchestrator.py`, and ~19 more raw `agent.status = "terminated"` DB writes scattered across `src/mcp/api.py`, `src/mcp/server.py`, `src/mcp/autopilot_api.py`, and `src/monitoring/*.py` each had to remember the task-reset step independently. The historical trace shows this happened in five distinct waves, each widening the scope of the same unfixed idea rather than centralizing it:

1. **`91a228c`** (2026-07-04) — the earliest and broadest sweep. Found 12 sites setting `status="terminated"` without clearing `Agent.current_task_id` (a *different* half of the invariant than task-reset) across `manager.py`, `orchestrator.py`, `api.py`, `autopilot_api.py`, `server.py`, and `monitor.py`, and patched all 12 in one commit — but this pass fixed only the agent-row half, not the task-reset half.
2. **`53e933a`** (2026-07-31) — added a safety-net query directly inside `terminate_agent` (`src/agents/manager.py`): after every raw field flip, re-query for any `Task` still pointing at `assigned_agent_id == agent_id` in `("assigned", "in_progress")` and reset it to `pending`. Commit message explicitly frames this as "closing the gap at the shared primitive instead of requiring every call site to remember it" — the first attempt at centralizing, but only inside the *one* primitive, not `terminate_agent_direct` or the ~15 raw-write sites elsewhere.
3. **`83e79ec`** (2026-08-04) — found the `53e933a` safety net itself incomplete: it only covered `assigned`/`in_progress`, missing `pending` tasks that still carried a stale `assigned_agent_id` ("hybrid pending state"). One-line filter extension.
4. **`27e8e88`** (2026-08-13) — audited all 19 raw writes plus the `terminate_agent_direct` primitive and found the same gap replicated at four more sites that never got `53e933a`'s treatment: `terminate_agent_direct` itself (`orchestrator.py`), `OrphanSessionReaper`'s workflow-inactive cleanup (`orphan_reaper.py`), `requeue_design` (`autopilot_api.py`), and the `stop_workflow` route (`server.py`). Each got its own copy of the same reset-loop, with its own regression test.
5. **`92caa82`** (2026-08-13, same day) — see §2 below; a sixth site (`_auto_restart_agent` in `monitor.py`) was still missing the reset, discovered via a live incident.

**Proper fix technique** (converged on by `53e933a`/`83e79ec`/`27e8e88`): after the agent-row fields are flipped, query `Task` by `assigned_agent_id == agent_id` filtered to `status IN (pending, assigned, in_progress)`, and for each: `status = "pending"`, `assigned_agent_id = None`. Applied as a **safety net at the primitive** (`terminate_agent`, `terminate_agent_direct`) so well-behaved callers that already reset their own task first are no-ops, but a caller that forgets is covered automatically — the technique is correct, but it was re-derived and re-applied at each of the four raw-write call sites individually rather than being made structurally impossible to skip.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the Agent Lifecycle Management section above cites `83e79ec`, `92caa82`, `27e8e88` as "the fourth time this exact class of bug has been found and fixed." The historical trace **adds a correction**: the actual count is at least five distinct fix commits, not three-going-on-four — `91a228c` (the original 12-site current_task_id sweep) and `53e933a` (the commit that *introduced* the stray-task safety net `83e79ec` later extended) both predate the three the analysis names, and both are root fixes of the same invariant, just scoped to a different half of it (`current_task_id` clearing vs. task-status reset) or a different location (the primitive itself, before `27e8e88` replicated it outward). The recommendation of a single shared termination primitive is well-supported by this trace: five independent fixes over six weeks, each expanding scope rather than centralizing, is exactly the failure mode a shared primitive is meant to close.

### 2. Reset-before-kill ordering: a dying agent's in-flight completion call racing the task reset

**The recurring problem.** Even after the task-reset step exists, *when* it runs relative to the tmux kill and the agent-row status flip matters. If `terminate_agent(agent.id)` runs (and its DB write commits) before the caller resets its own task, there's a window where `Agent.status == "terminated"` but `Task.status` is still `in_progress`/`assigned`. A separate, unrelated periodic sweep can observe exactly that combination and fail the task with a generic "terminated unexpectedly" reason — racing ahead of, and defeating, the caller's own subsequent (correct) task-status update.

**Instances found:**
- **`91699b1`** (2026-07-31) — `_detect_connection_errors` in `src/monitoring/monitor.py` called `terminate_agent()` first, then updated the task. Commit message: "Reordered to match the session-limit path exactly: update the task (pending+fallback or failed+reason) and commit first, then terminate the old agent." The fix physically moved the three `terminate_agent()` call sites in that function to *after* the task's `session.commit()`.
- **`92caa82`** (2026-08-13, same day as `27e8e88`) — `_auto_restart_agent` (Guardian's "ignored steering N times" restart path) had the identical bug, root-caused from a live incident: an `adversarial_review` agent had written a complete review with real findings to disk, but got marked `terminated` by this path while still finishing up; its own completion call was then correctly rejected (`verify_agent_authentication` rejects a terminated agent's calls by design), the task exhausted its retry budget, and the workflow failed silently while the agent's tmux session kept running to completion in the background, fully orphaned. Fix: reset the task to `pending` *before* killing the tmux session and marking the agent terminated.
- **`bc5c803`** (2026-08-02, between the two) — the context-overflow restart handler was written *correctly* from the start (reset task → commit → `terminate_agent` → `create_agent_for_task`), i.e. it proactively followed the ordering convention `91699b1` had established two days earlier rather than needing a fix. This is useful negative evidence: once the ordering convention existed anywhere in the codebase, new code sometimes did copy it correctly — the bug was specifically in *pre-existing* call sites that predated the convention.

**Proper fix technique:** reset-then-commit the `Task` row first (in the same DB session/transaction the caller already has open), and only call `terminate_agent()`/kill the tmux session afterward. This closes the race because by the time the agent row flips to `terminated`, the task row already reflects `pending` (or `failed`, if that's the intended terminal state) — there's no window where a concurrent sweep can see a `terminated` agent still pointing at a live-looking task.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the audit table above (row 16, `monitor.py:2580`) notes "task-ordering OK; `terminated_at` gap is pre-existing" for `_auto_restart_agent`, correctly crediting `92caa82`. The trace **adds nuance the analysis doesn't capture**: this was not a one-off ordering bug fixed once — it's the same race fixed independently at two call sites two weeks apart (`91699b1` for `_detect_connection_errors`, `92caa82` for `_auto_restart_agent`), and `91699b1`'s own commit message reveals it was itself following a pre-existing correct pattern ("the session-limit path") that just hadn't been applied everywhere. The ordering constraint is real and load-bearing but, like §1, was enforced by convention/copy-paste rather than a primitive that makes the wrong order impossible.

### 3. `restart_agent` as an accreting god-function: six feature-parity gaps against the create path, one a self-inflicted regression

**The recurring problem.** `create_agent_for_task` is the fully-featured, heavily-tested agent-launch path; `restart_agent` is a separate, ~400-line reimplementation of a subset of the same launch/delivery sequence. Every time `create_agent_for_task` gained a capability, `restart_agent` silently lacked it, because nothing shares the logic between them. Distinct bugs found and fixed at this one function over six weeks:

- **`d6feae2`** (2026-06-24) — `restart_agent` called `_create_tmux_session` without `working_directory`, so it fell back to `config.project_root`; every restarted agent launched in the wrong directory. Fix reconstructed a worktree path as `worktree_base / f"wt_{agent_id}"`.
- **`0c88c14`** (2026-06-29, five days later) — `d6feae2`'s own fix was wrong: worktrees are named after the *workflow*, not the agent, so `wt_{agent_id}` was a non-existent path, silently falling back to `project_root` again — the exact same symptom `d6feae2` was supposed to fix, reintroduced by its own patch. Corrected to use `wf.working_directory` directly (with an existence check). **This is the strongest single "re-broke, re-fixed the same call site" instance in this bucket.**
- **`a466de0`** (2026-06-25) — `restart_agent` launched the CLI via `send_keys` but skipped the create path's `_send_initial_prompt_with_retry` step entirely, so a resumed pi agent sat idle at its welcome screen with no task (observed live: an architecture_design agent stuck for hours at 0% context, MCP 0/1). Fix: after launch, wait for CLI boot, then deliver a "continue task X, don't redo committed work" message via the same retry sender the create path uses.
- **`f0d7172`** (2026-06-25, same day) — `restart_agent` rebuilt the launch command without `thinking_level`, so a restarted phase agent silently dropped to the global default instead of its configured per-phase budget. Fix: capture `restart_phase.thinking_level` alongside the already-fetched `restart_phase_name` and pass it through to `get_launch_command`.
- **`df1143a`** (2026-08-04) — a later refactor moved `restart_phase_name` resolution below the code that used it, causing a `cannot access local variable` crash on every restart. Fix moved the phase-resolution block back above its use site.
- **`9ba2c63`** (2026-08-09) — restarting an agent launched with `--agent` (a Claude Code subagent file) dropped the system prompt the same way the *initial* launch path had already been found to drop it; fix prepends `restart_system_prompt` to the restart message when `--agent` is in the launch command.

**Proper fix technique:** none of these were centralized — each is a point-fix re-deriving a piece of what `create_agent_for_task` already does correctly (worktree resolution, prompt delivery, thinking_level, phase_name resolution order, system-prompt inclusion). The durable fix, not yet done, is a shared "resolve worktree → resolve env/model → launch → deliver prompt" pipeline used by both `create_agent_for_task` and `restart_agent`, so a capability added to one is available to the other by construction.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the "Known fragile spots" above mentions in passing that `restart_agent` "duplicates large portions of the same launch/delivery sequence independently rather than sharing a launch-and-deliver helper," but doesn't quantify it. This trace **adds the concrete count and evidence**: six distinct fix commits against this one function over roughly six weeks, one of which (`0c88c14`) is a direct regression of its own immediately-preceding fix (`d6feae2`) — the clearest evidence in this whole bucket that an un-shared, hand-maintained code path degrades faster than it can be manually kept correct.

### 4. Restart-loop protection and false-positive restart triggers

**The recurring problem.** The Guardian/monitor's "is this agent stuck, should I restart it" heuristics initially had no cap and no sanity checks, so a crash-looping CLI or a misclassified-but-actually-fine agent could be restarted indefinitely or restarted while it was in fact still working / already finished.

- **`0509c90`** (2026-06-22) — root cause of "5-6 agents for a single phase": the monitor detected "agent exited to command line" and restarted immediately without checking whether the task was already `done`. Fix: check `task.status == 'done'` before restarting, at both the missing-tmux-session and agent-exited detection points.
- **`003ea27`** (2026-06-22, same day) — root cause of duplicate agents from a different angle: pi's TUI output doesn't look like traditional shell activity, so Guardian classified a genuinely-working agent as "stuck" 3 times in a row and auto-restarted it. Fix: check `agent.last_activity` — skip the restart if the agent was active within the last 5 minutes.
- **`e143b42`** (2026-06-24) — added the actual loop-protection cap: a new `Agent.restart_count` column, incremented on every restart; once `restart_count >= 3`, the agent is terminated and its task marked `failed` instead of being relaunched again, preventing infinite restart cycles when a CLI keeps crashing on launch. Same commit added `restart_count` reset-on-restart bookkeeping and garbled-TUI-output detection as a second restart trigger.
- **`91a228c`** (2026-07-04, part of the same sweep as §1) — found the restart-count-exceeded termination path in `manager.py` (introduced by `e143b42`) was itself missing `current_task_id` clearing — the invariant violation from §1 recurring even inside the loop-protection code added specifically to fix a different problem, ten days after that code was written.

**Proper fix technique:** the two same-day commits (`0509c90`, `003ea27`) are pre-restart sanity checks (don't restart if the task is already done; don't restart if the agent is demonstrably still active), while `e143b42` is the actual circuit-breaker (bounded retry count, terminal failure state past the cap). All three are necessary and complementary, not redundant — but their same-day proximity indicates the restart-trigger heuristics were under-specified when first shipped and had to be iteratively narrowed against real false-positive/false-negative incidents rather than designed up front.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the restart section above states the `restart_count >= 3` cap correctly and cites `e143b42`-era behavior descriptively, but doesn't discuss `003ea27`/`0509c90` as the precursor guards that made the restart trigger itself reliable enough for a cap to be meaningful. Worth noting as an addition: the cap alone wouldn't have fixed the "5-6 agents per phase" duplicate-agent incidents — those needed the two independent restart-avoidance guards.

### 5. Abandoned-workflow / zombie-pipeline detection: false positives and negatives in "is this actually dead" heuristics

**The recurring problem.** Several independent heuristics try to answer "is this workflow/pipeline actually dead, or just idle/done," and each shipped with an incomplete definition of "dead" that produced either false positives (killing something legitimately idle) or false negatives (an unrecoverable "zombie" state with no automated way back).

- **`f2cedb1`** (2026-07-07) — `start_pipeline` used to hard-reject with a 409 if `service.running` was `True`, even when the service was in a "zombie" state (no active agents/workflows despite claiming to run). Fix: detect zero active agents + zero active workflows and auto-stop the zombie service before starting fresh.
- **`83f0f78`** (2026-07-09, two days later) — `f2cedb1`'s own zombie check was itself a false positive: it fired even when the pipeline was legitimately idle because the design queue was empty (nothing wrong, just done). Fix: only classify as "zombie" when there are `pending`/`active` `AutopilotDesign` rows that *should* be getting processed; an empty queue with no active agents is correctly idle, not a zombie.
- **`23f73a8`** (2026-07-20) — a workflow that `_escalate_stale_active_workflows` marks `failed` as a hedge ("likely lost mid-flight across a backend restart") had no automated path back: every phase-advancement case requires `status IN (active, paused)`, so a `failed` workflow was invisible forever without a human clicking Resume. Worse, simply flipping status back to `active` wasn't safe if the shared worktree (`Workflow.working_directory`) was also gone, since agent creation would silently fork a disconnected, unmergeable worktree instead of hard-failing. Fix added `_recover_abandoned_workflows_missing_worktree`: rebuilds the shared worktree from the feature's own branch (which still carries every phase's real commits), reconnects `working_directory`, and resumes — capped by the stuck task's own `retry_count` so a genuinely-broken workflow still eventually stays failed for a human.
- **`c28d3b7`** (2026-08-04) — `_workflow_appears_abandoned` returned `True` even when every task in the workflow was `done`, because it checked "no non-terminal-status tasks exist" without separately confirming that meant "completed" rather than "abandoned." Fix: short-circuit to `False` when all tasks are `done`.

**Proper fix technique:** each of these is a narrowing of a boolean "is it dead" predicate against a specific counterexample discovered live, not a structural fix — the pattern is the same each time: (1) heuristic ships checking one condition, (2) a real scenario is found where that condition alone over- or under-fires, (3) an additional condition is added to the same function. `23f73a8` is the most substantial of the four because it also builds an actual recovery path (not just a better detector) for the false-positive case it was fixing.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** this sub-pattern lives adjacent to, but is distinct from, the `current_task_id`/`terminated_at` invariant §1–2 documents in detail — it's about workflow/pipeline-level liveness detection rather than the `Agent` row's termination fields. This cluster (zombie-pipeline / abandoned-workflow false positives, `f2cedb1`/`83f0f78`/`23f73a8`/`c28d3b7`) is not named by hash anywhere in the analysis above — worth flagging as a gap: four independent "is it actually dead" heuristics (zombie-pipeline-on-start, abandoned-workflow-escalation, abandoned-workflow-recovery, workflow-appears-abandoned) exist in different files (`autopilot_api.py`, `orchestrator.py` twice) with no shared definition of liveness, mirroring the same "multiple ad hoc implementations of one concept" theme documented for termination itself.

### 6. Workflow-termination status handling: three same-day fixes to one function

**The recurring problem.** `WorkflowTerminationHandler` (`src/workflow/termination_handler.py`) is the code that runs when a workflow is terminated (e.g., cancelled, or completed by a validated result) and cleans up its phase executions and status. On 2026-08-03, within roughly a 12-minute window (12:03, 12:11, 12:15 per commit timestamps), three separate commits progressively corrected the same function:

- **`d614705`** (12:03) — the handler unconditionally set `workflow.status = "completed"` on *every* termination, including outright cancellations. Fix: terminated workflows are marked `"failed"`, not `"completed"`, with a `status_reason`.
- **`b7853df`** (12:11) — fixing `d614705` exposed a deeper bug in the same function: `execution.status = "completed"` was being set unconditionally for *every* phase execution, even phases that never actually ran or had zero completed tasks — masking never-started phases as successfully completed, causing `_start_next_phase` to skip them (execution "already completed") and orphaning their pending tasks. Fix: only mark `completed` if the phase has `done` tasks; otherwise `failed` (for `in_progress` phases with no completed work, or `pending` phases that never started).
- **`a960d16`** (12:15) — `b7853df`'s fix was still incomplete: it marked a phase execution `"completed"` whenever `done_tasks > 0`, without checking whether *other* tasks in that same phase were still `pending`/`assigned`/`in_progress`. A phase with some done work and some still-pending work got marked `completed` anyway, orphaning the pending tasks (never dispatched, since the execution row now claimed the phase was finished). Fix: only mark `completed` when `done_tasks > 0 AND pending_tasks == 0`; otherwise leave the execution `in_progress` so the pending tasks can still be picked up when the workflow resumes.

**Proper fix technique:** the converged-on logic is: workflow-level status on termination is `failed` (not `completed` — termination isn't success), and phase-execution status must reflect actual task completion state at three levels of granularity (no work done → `failed`; some done, none pending → `completed`; some done, some still pending → stay `in_progress`) rather than a single unconditional assignment.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** not covered above (that section's termination discussion centers on the `Agent`-row invariant, not `WorkflowTerminationHandler`'s phase-execution status semantics). Worth flagging as its own finding: three sequential, same-hour fixes to one function is unusually strong evidence that the underlying state machine ("what does 'terminated' mean for a phase execution that's 0%, partially, or fully done") was under-specified rather than mis-implemented — each fix uncovered the next layer of the same missing specification rather than being an independent, unrelated bug.

### 7. Termination completeness beyond DB fields — process cleanup and "stop" scope, briefly

Two smaller but related clusters, both from 2026-06-18–19, worth a brief mention for completeness:

- **`c224b01`** — `terminate_agent`'s `tmux kill-session` didn't kill the CLI's actual child processes (opencode/claude/pi), which could linger after the tmux session was gone. Fix: after killing the session, `tmux list-panes` for pane PIDs and `kill -9 -- -{pid}` (negative PID, whole process group) each one.
- **`b390a00`** / **`9508c6d`** — `heph autopilot stop` iterated through three same-day states: first it marked the workflow `"completed"` on stop (wrong — stopping isn't finishing, corrected to `"pause"`), then it terminated only agents tied to autopilot's own workflows, missing any other active agent (`b390a00` widened the stop endpoint to terminate *all* `working`/`starting`/`idle` agents regardless of workflow association, and added a CLI-side fallback that re-terminates any still-active agents found after the API call).

Both are "termination isn't just a DB write" instances — process-group cleanup and scope-of-what-counts-as-"stop" — adjacent to, but distinct from, the `current_task_id`/`terminated_at` invariant that dominates §1–2.

## Bug-Fix History: Agent Session, tmux & Timeout Patterns

*Derived from a fresh read of 35 of the 87 `fix:` commits in this theme via `git show`, spread across 2026-06-17 through 2026-08-14, independent of the file-structure analysis above. That analysis covers the *current, converged* implementation of tmux session creation, `pipe-pane` transcript capture, and pane-PID-before-kill teardown. What follows is largely the multi-commit *history of arriving there* (each wrong turn and why it looked right at the time), plus several sub-patterns — pause-state fragmentation, stuck-detection signal corruption, retry-cap misclassification, session-limit false positives — that the file-structure analysis doesn't discuss at all.*

#### Transcript capture reliability

### 1. Carriage-return/spinner collapsing was rewritten three separate times in five days

**Problem:** TUI agents (Claude Code, pi) redraw progress lines with bare `\r`, not `\n`. Naively stripping `\r` destroys the spinner-collapse signal; naively keeping it duplicates every redraw as a new line; collapsing it wrong drops legitimate blank-line-adjacent output. This bounced back and forth because each fix was validated against a different observed symptom, not the general case.

**Fix arc:** `c71063a` (Jul 4) first strips `\r` outright as part of a broader ANSI-strip pass. `de85ccb` (Jul 9) reverses that specifically — preserves `\r` so a later pass can collapse it — but ships without the collapse logic working correctly. `669b8d4` (Jul 9, same day) rewrites the collapse algorithm on both backend and frontend: normalize any `\r` *not* immediately followed by `\n` into a line break first, then take the last `\r`-separated segment per resulting line, dropping empty segments (spinner noise). Two independent implementations (Python in `manager.py`, TypeScript in `RealTimeAgentOutput.tsx`) had to be kept in sync by hand.

**Commits:** `c71063a`, `de85ccb`, `669b8d4`.

**Doc comparison:** Not mentioned above — the file-structure analysis only describes the final, converged perl filter. This churn is worth flagging as evidence the CR-handling logic needs one shared implementation (currently duplicated frontend/backend) rather than a third rewrite next time a TUI changes its redraw style.

### 2. `pipe-pane`'s perl filter froze because it was line-buffered on input, not just output

**Problem:** The classic bug here isn't the escape-stripping regex (that part was right from `c71063a` onward) — it's that `perl -pe '...'` implicitly does `while(<>){...}`, which blocks reading a full line up to `\n` before it has anything to process, let alone flush. Since Claude Code's TUI redraws almost entirely via `\r` and cursor-positioning escapes rather than literal `\n`, a live agent's transcript could sit frozen at the byte offset of the *launch command's own trailing newline* for its entire multi-minute run — `tmux capture-pane` on the same session showed fresh output the whole time. An earlier autoflush fix (`BEGIN{$|=1}`) addressed the *output* buffering side and looked like it fixed the problem in testing, but never touched the *input* side that was the actual cause.

**Fix:** `be54004` replaced the `-pe` one-liner with an explicit `perl -e '... while (sysread(STDIN, my $buf, 65536)) {...}'` loop — `sysread` returns as soon as *any* bytes are available on the pipe (matching `pipe-pane`'s own delivery granularity), so every read pairs with an immediate `print`+flush. Documented tradeoff: a multi-byte escape sequence split across two `sysread` calls survives unstripped (cosmetic), traded deliberately against minutes of frozen scrollback.

**Commit:** `be54004`.

**Doc comparison:** The *end state* of this fix (the `sysread()` loop, `$|=1` autoflush) is already documented accurately in the Agent Lifecycle Management section above ("Transcript capture via pipe-pane"). This entry adds the specific race window and the fact that a prior autoflush-only fix looked sufficient but wasn't — useful context for why "explicit `sysread()` loop... not `-pe`'s implicit `while(<>)`" phrasing matters.

### 3. Live-agent output source oscillated between transcript log and live `capture-pane`, then converged on a stability-poll design

**Problem:** Three plausible sources exist for "what is this agent currently showing": the durable `pipe-pane` transcript file, a live `tmux capture-pane` snapshot, and (for terminated agents) a final `AgentLog` capture. Each has different failure modes — the raw transcript re-shows every intermediate `\r`-redraw as its own duplicate line; a naive `capture-pane` diff can't tell a still-streaming line from garbage; the transcript file can be empty if `pipe-pane` hasn't started yet.

**Fix arc:** `ea5f358` (Jul 7) switches live agents to `capture-pane` and reserves the transcript log for terminated agents only, reasoning that partial writes during active sessions garbled the log-based view. `150c2c4` (same day) adjusts to a one-time transcript load plus ongoing `capture-pane` polling. `2ad6630` (Jul 29) introduces the real solution: a `_poll_stable_transcript` method that only commits a pane line to the "clean" transcript once it's been byte-identical across `_STABILITY_CONFIRMATIONS` consecutive polls — originally 2, raised to 3 in this same commit after a real incident where pi briefly reserves a blank placeholder line before streaming real content in, and that blank state persisted across exactly 2 polls and got permanently committed (committed positions are append-only, never revisited — the "All checks passed! / EXIT: 0" from a ruff run was silently replaced by two blank lines). The fallback chain was also reordered: prefer a live unconfirmed `capture-pane` snapshot over the raw duplicated-line pipe-pane transcript when nothing has stabilized yet.

**Commits:** `ea5f358`, `150c2c4`, `2ad6630`.

**Doc comparison:** The analysis's "Known fragile spots" mentions the "five-level fallback chain" as a known code smell but doesn't detail the stability-poll mechanism or the blank-placeholder race. Worth adding: `N`-consecutive-poll confirmation before committing a transcript line is the general pattern for any TUI-diffing detector in this codebase (see also sub-pattern 7 below, which needed the same fix applied to Guardian's stuck-detection signature).

#### Pause, restart & resume races

### 4. Mid-launch abort race: an agent kept working after being "paused"

**Problem:** `create_agent_for_task` sleeps 25s after launch to let the CLI boot before typing the real prompt into the pane. That sleep has no way to notice a pause (or any other termination) that lands mid-sleep — the coroutine simply wakes up and delivers the prompt to an agent whose DB row was already flipped to `terminated`. This also had a second-order effect: the leftover "working" agent fooled `/autopilot/status`'s running-check into reporting the whole pipeline as still active, and `Feature.status` was never set to `"paused"` in the first place, so the UI showed "Active" even after everything had genuinely stopped.

**Fix:** `22178b1` adds a DB re-check for `status == "terminated"` immediately after the 25s sleep, before prompt delivery — aborts the launch and kills the stray tmux session if the agent was terminated in the meantime. Also fixes `pause_project_workflows` to set `Feature.status = "paused"` (previously missing entirely) and excludes `"paused"` workflows from the running-status active-agent query.

**Commit:** `22178b1`.

**Doc comparison:** The analysis's dispatch-flow §9 step ("mid-launch abort check") describes this check as an existing, working part of the flow and correctly attributes it to `22178b1`/a companion commit — this is new confirmation of the exact race window, not a contradiction.

### 5. Pause state (`paused_by`, `Feature.status`, workflow status) fragmented across independent call sites

**Problem:** As the analysis already observes, "no single pause primitive exists." The practical consequence, visible across a run of commits, is that each pause/resume surface implements its own subset of the invariant (set `wf.status`, set `wf.paused_by`, set `wf.paused_at`, set `Feature.status`), and it took several independent bug reports to fill in the gaps one call site at a time.

**Fix sequence:** `9aa2a19` — `pause_workflow_direct` set `wf.status = "paused"` but never `paused_by`/`paused_at`, leaving state inconsistent for anything that branches on `paused_by`. `ce0c4a7` — `_pause_feature_for_review` re-paused a feature for review even after it had already been approved, because it never checked `review_status` first. `bacaf6b` — two more reactivation paths (`_create_corrective_task`, `_resume_stuck_workflow_tasks`) force a workflow back to `"active"` with no check for `wf.paused_by == "user"`, found by auditing every other `wf.status = "active"` write site after a prior fix (`_try_auto_resume_paused_workflow`) closed the same gap elsewhere — `_create_corrective_task`'s version was worse, since it also immediately spawns a live agent, not just flips a status flag.

**Commits:** `9aa2a19`, `ce0c4a7`, `bacaf6b`, `22178b1` (sub-pattern 4).

**Doc comparison:** New/sharper. The analysis states the "no single pause primitive" observation as a structural finding but doesn't have this incident history — it's a concrete case for the same recommendation (applied by analogy from the termination-primitive case) that pause needs one write path, the same way termination does.

### 6. Repair/resume routines and a stale "system"-paused heuristic left workflows stuck paused indefinitely

**Problem:** Two distinct bugs converged on the same symptom — "Resume/Play does nothing, workflow stays paused forever." First, `_run_repair` never resumed the paused workflows it was auditing, so any repair action taken against a paused workflow was silently inert. Second, `_try_auto_resume_paused_workflow`'s guard was `wf.paused_by is not None`, but *every* real pause site sets `paused_by` to something non-`None` (`"user"`, `"budget"`, `"review"`, `"system"`) — making the guard equivalent to "always skip," i.e. dead code for the function's entire lifetime. The `"system"` value specifically is set by `_maybe_retry_failed_tasks`'s exhausted-retry-cap give-up — a heuristic judgment, not operator intent — and can go stale the instant a still-in-flight retry (dispatched via a separate, uncapped path) succeeds moments later. Observed live: a `security_review` task's final attempt started at 04:46:56 and succeeded at 04:52:12, but the workflow had been paused at 04:47:16 based on the task's prior failed state, leaving a `PhaseExecution` stuck `in_progress` for ~22 hours with no follow-on task ever created.

**Fix:** `57bd107` adds an explicit "resume all paused workflows for this design" step to `_run_repair`, before the completion check. `72365c2` reorders repair to resume-then-check-completion. `a333616` narrows the auto-resume guard to `wf.paused_by not in (None, "system")` — letting `"system"`-paused workflows through for reconsideration while still respecting `"user"`/`"budget"`/`"review"`, and clears `paused_by`/`status_reason` on the resume so the state doesn't relapse.

**Commits:** `57bd107`, `72365c2`, `a333616`.

**Doc comparison:** New. Neither the analysis nor the plan discusses the `paused_by` value space or the distinction between a deliberate operator pause and a heuristic system give-up that needs re-evaluation once its premise changes.

#### Stuck/timeout detection reliability

### 7. The detection *signal itself* got corrupted, silently defeating thresholds that looked correctly implemented

**Problem:** Three unrelated-looking bugs share one root cause: a detector that diffs raw tmux/pane output across polls is only as good as the assumption that identical visible content produces identical bytes. That assumption broke three separate ways. (a) `AgentManager._read_transcript_log` deliberately *keeps* SGR color codes when stripping other ANSI (so humans see color) — but `_mechanical_recovery_for_agent`'s frozen-output detector and `_detect_repetition_loop`'s line counter both compared that same colorized text across polls, and a TUI that re-emits color codes on every redraw (common) made two reads of an identical screen differ byte-for-byte, silently disabling both detectors for any agent whose stuck screen had colored text — one agent sat frozen 12+ minutes with zero recovery attempts before this was caught. (b) `_mechanical_recovery_for_agent`'s own nudge message gets echoed into the pane by most CLIs, so the poll immediately after sending a nudge almost always shows "changed" output — read as real progress, resetting the recovery-attempt counter to 0 every cycle, meaning `max_recov` (2) was never actually reachable; five-plus consecutive "Operation aborted" nudges fired for the same agent with no escalation. (c) A shell dropped into an unterminated-quote/paren continuation prompt (`dquote>`, from an agent's own prior Bash tool call) accepts keystrokes but never surfaces them to the CLI — every nudge just typed more garbage into the stuck shell and never reached the agent.

**Fix:** `17ec475` adds a shared `_strip_sgr()` helper applied before both the frozen-signature and repetition-loop comparisons. `f3d584a` re-baselines the stuck-signature to the *post-nudge* pane content immediately after sending it (not before), and makes the escalation condition symmetric with the nudge-trigger condition (`abort_frozen or frozen_for >= frozen_seconds`, not just the slower `frozen_seconds`) so exhausting `max_recov` via the fast 30s path doesn't then require waiting the full 300s before escalating. `18671bb` adds `_pane_is_wedged()` — regex-matches zsh/bash continuation-prompt variants on the pane's last non-blank line, sends `Ctrl-C` to break out before delivering any nudge, and re-checks once before falling back to best-effort delivery anyway.

**Commits:** `17ec475`, `f3d584a`, `18671bb`.

**Doc comparison:** New — none of this incident class (signal corruption defeating a correctly-thresholded detector) appears in either document. The general lesson — any pane-content diff needs its comparison normalized (strip color, account for self-echo, detect wedged input) before the threshold logic can be trusted — is a good addition to the analysis's fragile-spots list.

### 8. Guardian's LLM-analysis timeout fallback silently reported "aligned" forever; stuck-detection thresholds were also just miscalibrated

**Problem:** Guardian's trajectory analysis has a 90s LLM-call timeout that falls back to a benign default (`trajectory_aligned=True, needs_steering=False`) so a slow model call can't freeze the monitor loop — sound in isolation, but returning that default *unconditionally* meant an agent whose analysis kept timing out (observed: 4+ times over 12 minutes, on an agent that had hard-stopped on a model error) got reported "fine" on every single cycle, forever. Separately, the flat 15-minute stuck-detection / 10-minute nudge-delay defaults were simply too aggressive for how long agents legitimately run without producing visible output.

**Fix:** `17ec475` adds `GUARDIAN_TIMEOUT_ESCALATION_THRESHOLD` (3) — after 3 consecutive analysis timeouts for the same agent, the timeout pattern itself is treated as a stuck signal (`needs_steering=True, steering_type="stuck"`), feeding the existing nudge/auto-restart path instead of the neutral default; counter resets on any successful analysis. `c327dbe` raises `stuck_detection_minutes` 15→30, `guardian_nudge_delay_minutes` 10→15, and adds a `max_stuck_nudges` cap (5) — all made configurable via `hephaestus_config.yaml` per this project's `no-hardcoded-timeouts` convention.

**Commits:** `17ec475`, `c327dbe`.

**Doc comparison:** New. Neither document discusses the Guardian timeout-fallback trap or the specific threshold history.

#### Orphaned agents, session collisions & retry-cap misclassification

### 9. Agent DB rows outlived their tmux session or their workflow, in more than one shape

**Problem:** An `Agent` row can go stale relative to reality two ways — its tmux session dies out from under it (backend restart, manual `tmux kill-session`, crash) while the row still says `idle`/`working`; or its workflow stops being active while the agent row is still marked live. Both leave a phantom "busy" agent that duplicate-dispatch guards and retry logic treat as real, per this project's own `agent-termination` invariant.

**Fix:** `9641fec` (Jun 19, earliest fix in this cluster) adds a `_cleanup_orphaned_tmux_sessions` check that terminates agents whose task's workflow is no longer `active`/`running` — but does so with a raw `agent.status = 'terminated'` write that sets neither `terminated_at` nor clears `current_task_id`, an earlier instance of exactly the invariant-violation class flagged above as still-live today at `monitor.py:1809`. `95274ff` (Aug 4) adds the mirror-image detector, `_detect_orphaned_idle_agent`: an `idle` agent whose named tmux session no longer exists in `self.agent_manager.tmux_server.sessions` gets terminated and its task failed for retry — this path does set `terminated_at`.

**Commits:** `9641fec`, `95274ff`.

**Doc comparison:** `9641fec`'s raw termination write is worth flagging explicitly as a *second, earlier occurrence* of the same invariant-violation pattern the analysis's audit table catalogs at `monitor.py:1809` — reinforcing the point that this bug class has now recurred multiple times at different call sites because it's enforced by convention, not a single write path.

### 10. Session-ID reuse and stale tmux name collisions at launch

**Problem:** Claude Code's `--session-id`/`--resume` split each work exactly once — `--session-id` on a fresh UUID, `--resume` on an existing one — so any phase that intentionally reuses a session ID (e.g. `product_validation` resuming `product_requirements`'s session) always ate a guaranteed "already in use" error on the first attempt before an `||` shell fallback caught it. That error text also got fed raw into Guardian's LLM trajectory analysis, which sometimes misread it as a live problem and fabricated a bogus "fix your session conflict" steering message. Separately, the "kill any stale tmux session before creating a new one with the same name" path was silently dead code: it called `Server.get_by_id(session_name)`, but `get_by_id` filters by tmux's *internal* `$N` session identifier, not by name — passing a name always returned `None`, so the guard never killed anything and name collisions went unresolved.

**Fix:** `95c799c` adds `_claude_session_exists()` — a best-effort check against `~/.claude/projects/<sanitized-path>/<uuid>.jsonl` (mirroring Claude Code's own path-sanitization scheme) to pick which of `--session-id`/`--resume` to try *first*, keeping the `||` fallback for when the heuristic misses; also strips the known-benign "already in use" line via regex before Guardian's LLM sees it, leaving the transcript and viewer untouched. `a1b5752` replaces `get_by_id(name)` with `Server.kill_session(name)` at all three call sites that had the misuse (agent launch, validator spawn, a test helper). Related: `8ff642b` adds `design_id` to the session-ID fallback chain (`design_slug || design_id || feature_id`) after a workflow with none of the higher-priority identifiers fell through to `--no-session`, and pi exiting after each turn under `--no-session` produced a 10-restart loop — closed for good by `e609001`, which removes `--no-session` from pi's launch command entirely so pi stays interactive and the monitor can tell "running but stuck" from "exited."

**Commits:** `95c799c`, `a1b5752`, `8ff642b`, `e609001`.

**Doc comparison:** New — session-ID collision handling and the `get_by_id` misuse aren't mentioned in either document.

### 11. System-caused task failures wrongly counted against the agent retry cap; session-limit detection had its own false-positive problem

**Problem:** The retry-cap logic (`max_retry_count = 2`) exists to stop a genuinely broken task from looping forever, but several failure categories aren't the *agent's* fault at all — they're scheduling/resource issues that should retry indefinitely without burning the cap: tasks never dispatched to an agent ("Orphaned"), tasks the monitor terminated for inactivity ("Task stuck: no agent activity"), and Claude/OpenRouter session or spend-limit rejections. Each was added to the retryable-bypass list in a separate commit as each was independently discovered still triggering the "exhausted retries, pause the workflow" path. A related but distinct bug: the orphaned-*pending*-task check in `_create_phase_task` (a different code path — detecting a task that's been sitting `pending` with no `assigned_agent_id`) had no minimum-age floor, so it fired on any agentless pending task regardless of how recently it was created; two callers evaluating the same phase 11 seconds apart raced past each other, with the second one finding the first task still mid-dispatch (a normal few-second gap between row-commit and agent-attach), "helpfully" marking it a failed orphan, and spawning a full duplicate agent for the same phase. And the session-limit detector itself had a false-positive problem in the opposite direction: matching the generic fragments `"session limit"` and `"rate limit"` anywhere in the last 50 captured pane lines also matched those phrases appearing in the *task's own prompt text* (which legitimately discusses limits as subject matter), misclassifying a normal task as a session-limit failure.

**Fix:** `e9586a2` — orphaned tasks bypass the retry cap and don't increment `retry_count`, across three separate query sites (`_retry_failed_tasks`, `_case_in_progress_complete`, `_maybe_retry_failed_tasks`) that each had their own independent filter to patch. `bda565a` extends the same bypass to session/spend-limit failures via a shared `_limit_failure` lambda, and routes them to the fallback model on retry. `8322ed5` extends it again to "Task stuck" failures via a `_stuck_failure` lambda, in the same two call sites, noting explicitly these are system-detected terminations, not agent faults. `8b6aac7` adds a 1-minute `orphan_cutoff` age floor to `_create_phase_task`'s separate orphaned-pending-task check, matching the threshold `_case_in_progress_complete` already used elsewhere in the same file. `8e77d61` tightens session-limit detection from generic `"session limit"`/`"rate limit"`/`"too many requests"` fragments down to the exact Claude phrases `"you've hit your session limit"` and `"too many requests, please slow down"`.

**Commits:** `e9586a2`, `bda565a`, `8322ed5`, `8b6aac7`, `8e77d61`.

**Doc comparison:** New — retry-cap classification and the orphan-detection age-floor race aren't discussed in either document. Notable pattern across all three retry-cap fixes: each was added independently to 2–3 duplicated query sites rather than one shared "is this failure system-caused" predicate — a candidate for consolidation alongside the existing recommendations for the two duplicate-dispatch guards and the two termination primitives.

---

**Summary of doc-relationship:** Sub-patterns 2, 9 (partially) touch mechanisms already described at their *final* state in the Agent Lifecycle Management section above (pipe-pane sysread loop, CLAUDECODE unset, termination invariant) — this pass adds the specific race windows and incident history behind them. Sub-patterns 1, 3–8, 10, 11 are not covered by either existing document and represent genuinely new material: CR-collapse churn, the mid-launch pause-abort race, pause-state fragmentation across call sites, detection-signal corruption (color codes, self-echo, wedged shells), Guardian's timeout-fallback trap, session-ID collision handling, and retry-cap misclassification of system-caused failures.

## Bug-Fix History: Agent Dispatch, Prompt & Launch Mechanics Patterns

*Derived from a fresh read of the actual commit diffs behind 48 `fix:` commits in this theme, independent of the file-structure analysis above.*

### 1. MCP tool-name prefix churn — five separate registries drifting independently

**The problem.** Tool names exist in at least four independently-maintained places that must agree byte-for-byte: `src/mcp/server.py`'s `/tools` listing, its `_MCP_TOOLS` dispatch dict, `mcp/mcp_client.py`'s `@mcp.tool()` decorators (a separate stdio MCP process, no test coverage), and every prompt/YAML string that tells an agent what to type. Across five weeks the prefix convention flipped repeatedly — bare names → `heph_` → `hephaestus_` → bare again — and each flip touched only some of the four surfaces, so agents got "Unknown tool" or `heph_heph_create_task`-style double-prefix errors depending on which registry they hit that day.

- `ef438e8` (Jul 29) added `hephaestus_` via `@mcp.tool(name=...)` in `mcp/mcp_client.py` for 15 tools, because prompts/YAML already referenced the prefixed form but the registrations didn't.
- `8e4105d` (Jul 29, same day) discovered `server.py`'s **separate** `/tools` and `/tools/execute` endpoints still listed bare names — a second registry that the first commit never touched.
- `d50ebd8` (Jul 29) then swept seven more files — live nudge/steering messages sent to already-stuck agents, `.md` prompt examples, YAML prose — for the same bare-name bug, explicitly noting some tool names (`submit_result`, `spawn_agent`, `give_validation_review`) were left alone because "fixing a prefix on a name I can't confirm is correct risks introducing a new wrong name."
- `e44689c` (Aug 4) reversed course and stripped `heph_` back out of `config/prompts/system_prompts.yaml`.
- `137f12b` (Aug 11) discovered the adapter itself prepends the server name (`heph`) as a prefix on top of whatever the registry already returns, so `heph_`-baked-in names doubled to `heph_heph_create_task`; fixed by making the registry bare everywhere and adding a defensive `tool_name[5:]` strip in `execute_tool` for both prefixed and bare incoming calls.
- `bede479` (Jul 6, earliest in this set) is the root symptom of the same "which registry is authoritative" problem from a different angle: `update_task_status` existed as an HTTP endpoint but was never added to the MCP tool registry at all, so agents got `'agent_id' is a required property` — the handler didn't exist for that call shape, not a naming mismatch.

**The proper fix.** Not present in the commits themselves — each commit is a point patch to whichever surface was caught failing that day, never a consolidation. The durable fix is a single source of truth (one name list, or a decorator/registration step that generates the `/tools` listing, `_MCP_TOOLS` dispatch, and prompt-template tool names from the same declaration) so a prefix change is one edit, not a grep-and-pray across four file classes. `137f12b`'s defensive strip on the receiving end is the closest thing to a systemic fix in this set — accept both forms rather than depend on every caller agreeing.

**Commits:** `bede479`, `ef438e8`, `8e4105d`, `d50ebd8`, `e44689c`, `137f12b`.

**Doc comparison.** New. Neither the Agent Lifecycle nor the Worktree/CLI-Interface sections above mention this saga at all — it's a `src/mcp/server.py`/`mcp/mcp_client.py` problem, not something a targeted read of `cli_interface.py` or the dispatch flow would surface. Worth flagging given the git_commit_push saga's own narrative already elsewhere in this document is a symptom of "same policy encoded in N places" — this is the same failure mode in the tool-naming layer, just spread across five weeks instead of eight hours.

### 2. Model-fallback keystroke delivery — atomicity and role resolution

**The problem.** `model_fallback_keystrokes` sends literal pane input (not a chat message) to switch a frozen agent's CLI to a fallback model.
