Two independent failure modes recurred: (a) splitting the switch into two sends — `"/model"` then a wait then the search text — created a window where a busy/slow agent let the second send land as a normal queued chat message instead of picker input, since the picker hadn't actually opened yet (`123d740`: 80 attempts system-wide, 0 confirmed, 58 unconfirmed, 6 abandoned); (b) `fallback_model()` was hardcoded per CLI-subclass identity (`PiAgent` always reads `cli_model_fallback`, `ClaudeCodeAgent` always reads `secondary_cli_model_fallback`) rather than by which CLI is *currently* configured as primary vs. secondary, so swapping `default_cli_tool`/`default_fallback_cli_tool` silently broke the lookup (`4a79736`).

**The proper fix.** For (a): collapse to one atomic send. pi's `/model` accepts the search text as a trailing argument on the same line (`/model {name}`), pre-filtering the picker to a single match in one shot with no wait window for the agent to be mid-turn in — matching the one-line form `ClaudeCodeAgent` already used successfully. For (b): resolve the config key by **role** (`is_primary = agent.cli_type == config.default_cli_tool`, computed once and passed to a single shared, non-overridden `fallback_model(config, is_primary)`), not by CLI product identity — this is a base-class method now, not something each subclass duplicates its own copy of.

Downstream of (a) and (b), two more incidents surfaced once the switch started actually landing and got persisted:
- `bd1f856`: persisting the switch to `Agent.cli_model` (needed for UI display) meant a failed/unconfirmed switch left the agent permanently excluded from retry (stuck in the one-shot set) *and* blocked by its own optimistic DB write — fixed by reverting `Agent.cli_model` and clearing the one-shot set together on an unconfirmed-past-grace-period verdict.
- `0ae0553`: that retry-on-unconfirmed had no cap — one agent retried 40+ times over 7 hours, and because the "revert" only ever patched the DB record and never touched the live CLI session, one retry eventually landed on a different provider's catalog entry with no API key configured, breaking the session outright. Fixed with `MAX_FALLBACK_ATTEMPTS = 2`.
- `fba4bdc`: the *session-limit* fallback (a separate mechanism from the freeze-based one above) compared CLI tool only, so a same-tool/different-model reconfiguration (local vs. openrouter) never triggered fallback at all.

**Commits:** `123d740`, `4a79736`, `bd1f856`, `0ae0553`, `fba4bdc`, `b016a45`.

**Doc comparison.** Partially captured, materially thinner. The Per-CLI Comparison table in the Worktree/CLI Interface section notes pi's atomic `/model {name}` send and references "a two-step picker-based approach was tried and failed 58/64 times live" — but that's the *current-state* summary of `123d740` alone. The role-based resolution fix (`4a79736`), the retry-cap incident (`0ae0553`, an agent breaking its own session by drifting onto an unconfigured provider), and the persist/revert race (`bd1f856`) are not mentioned anywhere in the document — these are exactly the kind of CLI-integration timing bugs a targeted-read pass would miss.

### 3. Env var export/readback races at shell and tmux-pane startup

**The problem.** Two distinct timing races, both rooted in "the orchestrator doesn't actually know the shell/pane is ready to accept input, it just waits a fixed guess." `2321575`: `new_session()` returns before the pane's shell finishes its own startup (sourcing `.zshrc`, prompt theming, etc.); sending `export FOO=...` into that window mid-init can corrupt the first bytes (`export FOO=` arriving as `eexport FOO=`), silently failing and leaving the agent without its `HEPHAESTUS_AGENT_ID` etc. and without the agent-safe-bin `rm` guardrail on `PATH`. `b016a45` (earlier, cruder version of the same problem): fixed-duration `asyncio.sleep(0.1)` between exports and `sleep(1.0)` after, tuned by guesswork rather than an actual readiness signal.

**The proper fix.** Replace fixed sleeps with an actual readiness check: `_wait_for_shell_ready` polls `capture-pane` for two consecutive stable (identical, non-empty) reads before the first `send_keys`, capped by a timeout so a pane that never stabilizes doesn't block agent creation forever. Layered on top, `_export_env_vars_and_verify` writes a uniquely-tagged sentinel echo (`echo "__ENVCHECK_<uuid>__:$KEY"`) after exporting and greps the captured pane for it — a real read-back verification, not an assumption — and retries the whole export batch once on a failed match before giving up and launching anyway (logged, not silently swallowed).

Separately, `860a805` addresses the same "agent missing its own identity" failure mode from the opposite direction: rather than trust the model to always pass `agent_id`/`task_id`/`workflow_id`/`phase_id` explicitly on every MCP tool call (observed live: a model called `hephaestus_save_memory` without `agent_id` twice in a row despite the rendered prompt example showing it filled in — non-compliance, not a template bug), inject every identifier into the spawned agent's tmux environment and have `mcp/claude_mcp_client.py`'s tool functions fall back to `os.environ.get("HEPHAESTUS_AGENT_ID")` etc. when a param is omitted, only hard-failing if both the explicit arg and the env fallback are absent.

**Commits:** `b016a45`, `2321575`, `860a805`.

**Doc comparison.** Partially captured. The Agent Lifecycle Management section notes "env vars are exported into the pane and read back for verification, retries once on a failed readback" — an accurate current-state description of `2321575`'s end state — but doesn't narrate the shell-startup-corruption mechanism (`eexport FOO=`) or the fixed-sleep precursor in `b016a45` that made it necessary. `860a805`'s env-var-as-fallback-for-model-noncompliance pattern isn't mentioned.

### 4. Prompt content silently dropped — wrong attribute name, missing template placeholder, unused feature

**The problem.** Three separate ways prompt content silently failed to reach the agent, none of them raising loudly: (a) `5e58021` — `format_initial_message` referenced `phase_ctx.phase_definition.name`, but `PhaseContext`'s real field is `phase`; the `AttributeError` was caught and logged, not raised, so *every* phase-agent prompt silently lost its entire phase-context section (current phase description, `all_phases` list) for an unknown period, only found via repeated log lines across many `phase_id`s over an hour of a real run. (b) `91efbe6` — every phase YAML's `additional_notes` field (the actual detailed step-by-step instructions — schema examples, "CRITICAL PATH RULE" sections, `security_review`'s mandatory ASH scan step) was never wired into any of the three prompt-assembly templates at all; confirmed live that a `security_review` agent's rendered system prompt was 3,286 chars with zero mentions of "ash" against a 10,755-char `additional_notes` source. (c) `26ac785` — the review-feedback endpoint wrote to `TaskPromptOverride` but `format_initial_message` never read that table, so human review feedback silently never reached the re-dispatched agent on iteration. (d) `96e0d8a` — when Claude Code launches via `--agent` (a subagent file), the CLI doesn't apply `--append-system-prompt` at all; the agent file alone carries only minimal identity instructions, so safety rules/tool descriptions/memory context were dropped entirely unless the caller specifically detects this launch mode and prepends the full system prompt into the initial chat message instead.

**The proper fix.** Same shape each time: find the *one* choke point all prompt variants funnel through and fix it there rather than patch each template separately. `91efbe6` is the clean example — `PhaseContext.to_prompt_context()` is the single call all three templates (`phase_agent_instructions`, `phase_agent_resumed_instructions`, `non_phase_agent_instructions`) share, so appending `additional_notes` there (scoped only to the *current* phase, verified by a regression test asserting other phases' notes don't leak) fixes all three at once. For (a), the actual lesson is that a caught-and-logged exception in a prompt-assembly path is worse than a crash — it produces a silently degraded prompt with no operator-visible signal beyond a log line easy to miss.

**Commits:** `5e58021`, `91efbe6`, `26ac785`, `96e0d8a`, plus the closely-related tool-signature mismatches `e860b7b` and `168e324` (prompt examples that told agents to call `hephaestus_create_task(description=..., phase=N)` / omit `agent_id`, when the real MCP schema requires different parameter names — agents copy the example verbatim, so a wrong example is load-bearing, not cosmetic).

**Doc comparison.** Partially captured, only for (b). The Phase Management section's `PhaseContext.to_prompt_context()` discussion references `additional_notes` and explicitly cites "the docstring explicitly flags this as a previously-missing wire-up (an agent had 'no idea an ASH automated scan step existed')" — this is `91efbe6`, already documented. The `phase_definition`/`phase` attribute bug (`5e58021`), the `TaskPromptOverride` dead-letter (`26ac785`), the `--agent`-mode system-prompt drop (`96e0d8a`), and the prompt-example/schema mismatches (`e860b7b`, `168e324`) are not mentioned — all new.

### 5. CLI-specific launch flag and prompt-delivery mechanics (per-CLI quirks, found the hard way)

**The problem.** Each CLI product has its own undocumented rejection of the "obvious" launch approach, discovered only by watching real sessions hang or garble:

- `dbb6e57`: pi's `-p` flag combined with `--approve` hangs *specifically inside tmux* (works fine outside it) — the root cause of agents rendering a few TUI characters then freezing with garbled repeated text ("king Your working Your workedYour working..."), which the monitor would detect as frozen and restart, only to hit the identical hang again on the fresh session. The long-form `--print` flag doesn't have the bug; `-p`/`--print` are otherwise documented as equivalent.
- `c8ac161`: Codex's launch command originally passed the system prompt via `--system '<escaped prompt>'` on the command line; large prompts with quotes/special characters broke shell escaping, so the tmux session would start but the agent never actually initialized. Fixed by dropping the system prompt from the launch command entirely and sending it after initialization via the same chunked interactive-message path already used for Claude/Droid (2500-char chunks), rather than trying to get shell-escaping right for an unbounded-size string.
- `8152bcb`: pi's default launch flags triggered interactive permission prompts (AGENTS.md loading, session-persistence prompts) that a headless orchestrator can't answer — fixed with `--no-context-files --no-session`, plus pre-seeding `trust.json`.
- `9457f40`: Claude CLI launched without `--mcp-config`, so it never loaded the MCP tool definitions at all — agents had no `update_task_status`/`save_memory` etc. available regardless of what the prompt told them to call.
- `af2b937`: pane-output parsing filtered `Elapsed ...s` timing lines but missed the equivalent `Took ...s` variant, plus raw shell-prompt lines (`~/path (branch)`) and empty `$ ...` command echoes — all three kept polluting the "is this agent stuck" signature comparison used by the freeze-detection logic.

**The proper fix.** No single unifying technique — each is a CLI-specific empirical discovery (confirmed live in the commit messages, e.g. "`--approve` + `-p` hangs, `--approve` + `--print` works" for pi) captured as a comment/regex/flag change in that CLI's own subclass or in the shared chunked-delivery dispatch. The generalizable lesson is that `AgentManager`'s chunked-delivery path (later folded into the polymorphic `needs_chunked_delivery` hook per the file-structure analysis's description) is the safe default for any CLI whose launch-time system-prompt flag has size/escaping limits — Codex's fix effectively moved it into that bucket rather than inventing a Codex-specific workaround for the escaping itself.

**Commits:** `dbb6e57`, `c8ac161`, `8152bcb`, `9457f40`, `af2b937`.

**Doc comparison.** Partially captured, current-state only. The Per-CLI Comparison table documents `needs_chunked_delivery`, `NONE`/`FLAG`/`MESSAGE`/`AGENT_FILE` `prompt_delivery` modes, and per-CLI launch flags including `--mcp-config if present` for Claude — but as a snapshot of what the code does today, not the history of why (it doesn't mention the pi `-p`-hangs-in-tmux discovery, the Codex shell-escaping failure that motivated moving it into the chunked-delivery bucket, or the missing-`--mcp-config` incident that left agents toolless). This is exactly the "targeted read of current code vs. tracing historical fix commits" gap this bugfix-history pass is meant to close.

### 6. Dispatch/launch races against a task's own lifecycle

**The problem.** Two incidents where an in-flight agent launch didn't know the ground had shifted under it. `0fe3573`: the abort-check partway through CLI initialization only looked at the *agent* row's status (`"terminated"`) — but a task can be cancelled (marked duplicated/failed, or reassigned to a different agent by a competing dispatch attempt) without that specific agent ever being individually terminated. Observed live: one task cycled through five separate agent launches in a row, each surviving the CLI-init wait and delivering its prompt anyway, because each queued fallback-model dispatch attempt got a fresh `agent_id` the check had never seen terminated. `8e18cc6`: the spend-limit fallback re-dispatch called `create_agent_for_task(..., enriched_data={})` without the required `memories`/`project_context` kwargs (no defaults) — every invocation raised `TypeError`, silently caught by a broad `except`, so the fallback path was **100% non-functional** and every spend-limit hit degraded to permanent task failure instead of the intended re-dispatch to a different CLI/model.

**The proper fix.** For `0fe3573`: re-fetch the *task* fresh at the same checkpoint (not the possibly-stale `task` parameter captured before the wait) and check both `status in TaskStatus.TERMINAL` and `assigned_agent_id != agent_id` — either signals another dispatch attempt already won the task, independent of whether this specific agent was ever marked terminated. For `8e18cc6`: build the same `enriched_data`/`memories`/`project_context` the existing `_recreate_agent_with_new_approach` path already constructs, rather than passing empty/missing values into a function whose signature requires them — and note the broad `except` around the dispatch call is what let a 100%-failure-rate bug ship silently; the fix also tightened the accompanying test to assert the re-dispatch actually produces a `"pending"` task, not just that `terminate` was called once (the old test passed identically whether the fallback worked or not).

**Commits:** `0fe3573`, `8e18cc6`.

**Doc comparison.** New. The Agent Lifecycle Management section's "Task dispatch flow" documents the CLI/model concurrency gate (`resolve_cli_model_dispatch`) but not this specific stale-agent-vs-task-lifecycle race, nor the spend-limit-fallback-was-a-no-op incident — both are recent (Jul 24, Aug 14) fixes to the dispatch path's edge cases that a snapshot read of current code wouldn't surface as "this used to be completely broken."

### 7. MCP disconnection recovery — from silent nudging to escalation with reset

**The problem.** `1563663` (earliest): when the tmux status bar showed `MCP: 0/N servers`, the existing mechanical-recovery nudge just sent the standard "you appear stuck" text message — an agent with a disconnected MCP client can't act on that, since the tools it needs to respond (`update_task_status` etc.) aren't the problem, but sending it more *chat* text isn't a fix either. `efa1955`: even after `1563663` added a reconnect attempt, the monitor could nudge a disconnected agent indefinitely with no cap and no fallback — an agent stuck in a `Working...` spinner loop with MCP down couldn't process the text nudge at all, since the spinner absorbs keyboard input as chat rather than a command.

**The proper fix.** `1563663`: before the nudge, send an actual reconnect sequence into the pane (`Escape` to break any spinner, `/mcp`, `Ctrl+R`, `Escape` again, with sleeps between each to let the CLI process them) and append "MCP was disconnected and has been reconnected" context to the nudge message so the agent knows why it's being told to check its tool access. `efa1955` layered a hard escalation on top: send `Escape` before the reconnect nudge specifically to break the spinner/loop (not just as part of the reconnect sequence), track a per-agent nudge count, and after 3 failed nudges, stop nudging entirely — terminate the agent and reset its assigned tasks to `pending` so the pipeline can retry with a fresh session, rather than nudging a session that has structurally proven it can't recover. Nudge count resets to zero the moment MCP is observed reconnected.

**Commits:** `1563663`, `efa1955`.

**Doc comparison.** New/not directly addressed. The Monitoring section's mechanical-recovery description lists MCP disconnect as one of ~13 cheap detectors in the current mechanical-recovery sweep, but doesn't narrate the escalation-after-N-nudges design or the spinner-breaking `Escape`-before-nudge ordering that `efa1955` introduced as a fix to a previously-unbounded nudge loop.

## Bug-Fix History: Goto, Retry & Arbitration Patterns

*Derived from a fresh read of the actual commit diffs behind 39 `fix:` commits in this theme (2026-06-20 through 2026-08-13), independent of the current-code read in the Orchestrator Core / Phase Management sections above.*

### 1. In-memory budget counters reset on every fresh orchestrator instance

**Problem.** `WorkflowOrchestrator.total_gotos` and `WorkflowOrchestrator.phase_retry_counts` are plain Python attributes on the pure gate-evaluator object. That looked fine in isolation, but production never reuses one `WorkflowOrchestrator` instance across calls — `task_completion_service.py`'s `fire_spec_gate_if_ready` and `autopilot/orchestrator.py`'s periodic sweep both construct a brand-new `PhaseManager()` (hence a fresh, uncached orchestrator) on nearly every `mark_phase_complete` call. An in-memory counter on that object therefore silently resets to 0 on every single evaluation. The bug looked plausible because the code logged `"gotos=1/3"` on every call — it just never got past 1, so `max_total_gotos` and `eval_point.max_retries` never actually fired and a failing gate could oscillate between two phases forever (live: `scope_review` ↔ `product_requirements`).

**Proper fix.** Persist the counter on the DB row the evaluation is already scoped to, and sync it into/out of the orchestrator instance around each `evaluate()` call rather than trusting the object to retain state across calls it was never guaranteed to survive: `Workflow.total_gotos` (new column) synced in `PhaseManager.mark_phase_complete`, and the identical treatment one week later for `Phase.retry_count` once the same gap was found in the sibling dict. The `tasks.retry_count` case (`2f7437c`) is the same root defect one layer down — `attempt_recovery`'s "stop after 2 retries" guard read `retry_count` from `get_tasks()`'s dict, but no such column existed, so `task.get("retry_count", 0)` always returned 0 and two `architectural_review` tasks retried every ~60s indefinitely against a deleted worktree.

**Representative commits:** `b3178ee` (2026-07-06, `total_gotos`), `bff31a3` (2026-07-07, `phase_retry_counts` → `Phase.retry_count`), `2f7437c` (2026-07-07, `tasks.retry_count`).

**Relation to existing analysis.** New detail, not previously captured. The analysis doc's Phase Management section describes `WorkflowOrchestrator` as "a pure condition evaluator" with "no DB access," which is accurate for the *current* code but obscures that this statelessness was the direct cause of a real infinite-loop incident — the counters look pure only because they're now synced in from the DB by the caller on every call, a pattern that had to be independently discovered twice (goto, then retry) three days apart by the same person applying the same fix to the sibling field.

### 2. A persisted budget that still can't be given a fresh start

**Problem.** Once `total_gotos` was actually persisted (sub-pattern 1), it introduced a new failure mode: the counter never decreases on its own, and `_trigger_arbitration`'s per-phase arbitration cap (`max_arbitrations_per_phase = 3`) counted historical arbitration `Task` rows *all-time*, since those rows are never deleted. A workflow that exhausted either budget stayed permanently unrecoverable via the UI's Retry/Rerun action: clicking it flipped `Workflow.status` back to `"active"`, but the very next evaluation instantly re-exceeded the *same* already-exhausted limit and re-failed with zero real attempt in between. This traces back to an earlier, structurally identical problem at the `monitor.py` layer, before `orchestrator.py`'s `_create_phase_task` existed: the first version of a phase re-entry bound counted *all* monitor-created tasks including successful `continue`s, and the orchestrator loop didn't even recognize `"paused"` as a terminal status, so bounding out didn't stop the workflow.

**Proper fix.** `_resume_interrupted_workflows`' on-demand-retry (`reactivate=True`) branch now resets `total_gotos = 0` and stamps a new `Workflow.gotos_reset_at` column; `_trigger_arbitration`'s cap query filters `Task.created_at > gotos_reset_at` so a retried workflow's arbitration cap resets too instead of counting pre-retry attempts forever (`NULL` preserves all-time counting for never-retried workflows). The earlier `monitor.py`-era fix narrowed its own count to `Task.action.in_(["retry", "goto"])` via a new `Task.action` column (excluding `"continue"`), and taught the orchestrator's status check to exit on `"paused"` as well as `"completed"`/`"failed"`.

**Representative commits:** `46f8b0c` (2026-08-11, `gotos_reset_at`), `a89e256` (2026-06-25, first bound), `a6c097f` (2026-06-25, same-day review fixing 3 gaps in that bound).

**Relation to existing analysis.** New — the analysis doc's `max_total_gotos`/`max_arbitrations_per_phase` description is current-code-accurate but doesn't mention that "budget exhausted" and "workflow retried" are two states the code had to be taught to reconcile explicitly; without `gotos_reset_at` the Retry button in the UI is a no-op disguised as an action.

### 3. Arbitration silently could never run at all — a live incident spanning 30+ hours

**Problem.** `_trigger_arbitration` (the handler for `action="arbitrate"`, i.e. goto/retry budget exhausted) inserts a `Task` with `created_by_agent_id = ARBITRATION_CREATED_BY` and creates an `Agent` with `agent_type="arbitration"`. Neither value was ever legal: `Task.created_by_agent_id` is a real `ForeignKey("agents.id")` with no corresponding `Agent` row ever created for the sentinel, and `Agent.agent_type` has a `CHECK` constraint (`'phase', 'validator', 'result_validator', 'monitor', 'diagnostic', 'orchestrator'`) that never included `"arbitration"`. With `PRAGMA foreign_keys=ON`, every single dispatch raised `IntegrityError`, caught by a catch-all and logged only at `DEBUG` — invisible at the default log level. The bug looked plausible in tests specifically because `test_advance_phases.py`'s own autouse `_seed_sentinel_agents` fixture pre-created the row every test needed and production never did, so the whole suite passed against a condition production never actually satisfied. Live impact: one workflow re-evaluated to `"arbitrate"` on every ~15-20s sweep tick for over 30 hours — 1180+ failed attempts, `total_gotos` climbing the whole time, zero arbitration tasks ever created, and the exhaustion cap never engaging because it only counts tasks that don't exist. A third, independent gap in the same feature: `fire_spec_gate_if_ready` (the synchronous completion-path gate handler) checked `action in ("already_completed", "goto", "continue")` and silently fell through for `"arbitrate"` — `_fire_phase_transition` (the sweep's equivalent handler) already handled it correctly, but the synchronous path never got the same treatment, so a phase stuck needing arbitration re-hit this exact leak on every task completion (1100+ times over ~30 hours on one workflow).

**Proper fix.** Three sequential live-incident fixes in the same afternoon: `_trigger_arbitration` gets-or-creates its own sentinel `Agent` row before the `Task` insert (mirroring `server.py`'s existing pattern for its own `created_by_agent_id`); the `Agent`'s `agent_type` is set to `"diagnostic"` instead of `"arbitration"` — not a workaround, since `prompt_builder.py`'s `format_initial_message` already treats `"diagnostic"` and `"arbitration"` identically, so this satisfies the constraint with zero prompt-building change (arbitration tasks are still identified via `Task.created_by_agent_id`, unaffected); `fire_spec_gate_if_ready` gets the missing `elif result.get("action") == "arbitrate":` branch that actually invokes `_trigger_arbitration`. Separately, `903d76a` fixed the cap itself discarding a *correct* decision: the cap counted raw attempts, not convergence, so 3 consecutive arbitrations that all independently reached `"continue"` against an unchanged, clean `review.md` still failed the workflow — the 3rd arbitration's own reasoning noted it was being asked the same already-settled question a third time. Fix: before failing on cap-exceeded, check whether the most recent arbitration task is already `"done"` with a decision that was never processed (e.g. `_maybe_resolve_arbitration` hadn't reached it yet this tick) and resolve it instead of discarding it.

**Representative commits:** `d0f7411` (2026-07-31, FK), `6b37cb7` (2026-07-31, CHECK constraint, confirmed live minutes after deploying the FK fix), `fd926cc` (2026-07-31, missing `"arbitrate"` branch in the sync path), `903d76a` (2026-08-11, cap discards converged decision).

**Relation to existing analysis.** Sharper than the current-code read. The Orchestrator Core section already documents *why* arbitration agents use `agent_type="diagnostic"` ("not a real `arbitration` type, since `Agent.agent_type`'s CHECK constraint doesn't include one") — but frames it as a design decision, not as the third of a three-bug chain where arbitration was **completely non-functional in production for an unknown period before 2026-07-31**, invisible because every failure was swallowed at DEBUG and every test passed against a fixture that papered over exactly the condition production always hit. The doc doesn't currently mention the FK bug, the sync-path gap, or the cap-discards-convergence bug at all — all three are new findings from the diffs, not visible from reading current code alone (the current code is, after all, already fixed).

### 4. Task-level retry-cap exhaustion not correctly propagated to phase/workflow status

**Problem.** `_case_in_progress_complete` (the sweep's per-phase state-machine step) went through several rounds of the same underlying defect: a phase containing both `"done"` and `"failed"` tasks was treated inconsistently depending on which branch reached it first. Early versions only retried failed tasks when `failed_count == total_count` (all-failed) — a phase with some done and some failed tasks just got marked complete, silently dropping the failed work (`0a4e9d4`, `dcb631f`). Once retry was fixed to cover the mixed case, a phase where retries were genuinely exhausted (past the retry cap) was left `"in_progress"` forever, since nothing flipped `PhaseExecution.status` — blocking `_case_completed_with_successor` from ever advancing (`6b021be`). The worst variant: once `execution.status = "failed"` finally got set, the code still fell straight through into the "phase complete, fire transition" logic a few lines below — and `_fire_phase_transition`'s engine evaluation reads the *failed task's own stale self-reported action/completion data* (e.g. `"continue"`, written by the agent before an output validator rejected it), not `execution.status`. Live: `architectural_review` exhausted its retry cap on a genuine frontmatter-schema defect, and the pipeline advanced straight to `qa_validation` as if the review had passed (`acbf2bc`). A parallel gap existed for tasks stuck in `"pending"` (never even reaching `"failed"`) past the retry cap — invisible to a sweep that only scanned `"failed"` tasks (`ef16bd5`). And a workflow that had been paused as `"system-exhausted"` had no automated path back even after the underlying condition changed (`19620e5`).

**Proper fix.** Each fix narrowed the gap by one case rather than restructuring the whole check: retry failed tasks directly whenever `done_count > 0` regardless of whether *all* tasks failed; when all failed tasks are past cap, set both `execution.status = "failed"` **and** `wf.status = "failed"` with a `status_reason`, then `continue` past the transition-firing code instead of falling into it (mirroring `_trigger_arbitration`'s own exhausted-budget handling: fail explicitly, don't continue); sweep `"pending"` tasks with `retry_count >= max_task_retries` into `"failed"` so they're visible to the same cap logic; and widen `_retry_exhausted_paused_workflows` to also pick up `paused_by == "system-exhausted"` workflows that have failed tasks in an `in_progress` phase (conditions may have changed since exhausting).

**Representative commits:** `0a4e9d4` (2026-08-03), `dcb631f` (2026-08-03, same day), `6b021be` (2026-08-03, same day), `ef16bd5` (2026-08-04), `acbf2bc` (2026-08-04), `19620e5` (2026-08-03).

**Relation to existing analysis.** Confirms and sharpens an existing observation. Section 7's "Known Fragile Spots" already flags `_case_in_progress_complete`'s "done + failed coexist" branch as reimplementing retry-filtering logic a third time (a code-smell finding from the current code). What the diffs add: this wasn't one messy function written once — it's **six separate live-incident fixes over two days** each patching one more gap in the same state combination, which is a much stronger argument for the doc's own Refactor Observation #2 ("task-creation claim + retry logic is three parallel implementations... should be one function") than "the code looks duplicated" alone.

### 5. Goto/retry target resolution: stale cross-cycle lookups and ignored explicit targets

**Problem.** A phase revisited via goto reuses the same `phase_id` across cycles, and multiple independent "what happens after this phase" lookups queried `Task` history for that `phase_id` without scoping to the *current* cycle — so they could find a task from cycles (or days) earlier that has nothing to do with the current pass. `_start_next_phase`'s "last done task" lookup, used to find a recorded goto/retry target, was unscoped: a *synthetic* completion (e.g. the review-run cap's clean synthetic result, which closes a phase via `mark_phase_complete` without creating a new `Task` row) left the "last done task" pointing at whatever task last genuinely ran — live, `qa_validation` hit its cap, scored a clean synthetic pass, but the only "done" task on record was a `goto`/`development` tag from 2 days earlier, and the pipeline resumed at `development` instead of advancing forward (`22aa424`). A second, distinct instance of the same underlying issue: `_case_completed_with_successor`'s crash-recovery self-heal picked the successor purely by phase order, ignoring that the completing task may have recorded a different, more specific `action_target_phase` — a goto's own stale-reset resets every phase *at or after its own target* back to `"pending"`, including phases a *later* goto deliberately skips over, so "next pending phase by order" picked up a phase (`architectural_review`) that a subsequent goto had intentionally bypassed, dispatching a redundant re-review on every single fix cycle (`e596533`). Once explicit-target-skipping was correctly honored, the phases jumped over were left at whatever stale `"pending"` status an earlier reset left them in, forever indistinguishable from genuinely unstarted work — `derive_workflow_status`'s completeness check then saw the workflow as permanently incomplete even after the feature shipped (`dd7bf80`). Underlying all of these, the earliest version of `_start_next_phase` didn't even treat a `"completed"` next phase as advanceable (only `"pending"`), causing premature workflow completion after any goto reconvergence (`8d6577d`) — and a task's `action`/`action_target_phase` (the field all of the above reads) was itself being unconditionally cleared by `_maybe_retry_failed_tasks` on every retry, discarding the "resume at X once done" memory a `"failed"` task can only have gotten from its own creation-time tagging, since it never reached `"done"` to receive a post-completion badge (`26f498c`).

**Proper fix, by call site.** Scope the "last done task" query to `Task.created_at >= current_execution.started_at` (reset whenever the phase (re)starts), matching the scoping `_case_in_progress_complete` already used for its own cycle-tasks query (`22aa424`). Check the completing phase's own most recent done task for a recorded `action`/`action_target_phase` *before* falling back to order-based successor selection (`e596533`). Mark intermediate `PhaseExecution` rows `"skipped"` when a jump passes over them — but only if still `"pending"`, never downgrading a genuinely `"completed"` phase from an earlier pass (`dd7bf80`). Treat `"completed"` as an advanceable next-phase status, not just `"pending"` (`8d6577d`). Stop unconditionally clearing `action`/`action_target_phase` on task retry — a `"failed"` task can only have gotten those fields from creation-time tagging, so there is no stale post-completion badge to clear (`26f498c`).

**Representative commits:** `22aa424` (2026-07-23), `e596533` (2026-07-20), `dd7bf80` (2026-08-05), `8d6577d` (2026-06-20), `26f498c` (2026-07-23, same day as `22aa424`).

**Relation to existing analysis.** New, and a sharper synthesis than the current-code read supports. The Phase Management section describes goto/retry target resolution as three static mechanisms (linear-next, conditional-gate, explicit-re-entry-target) without noting that the interaction *between* them (an explicit target recorded on a stale cross-cycle task, or a jump silently orphaning skipped phases' status) was the actual source of five separate live incidents across seven weeks. This is structurally the same class of finding as the doc's own "GOTO/rewind stale-`PhaseExecution` reset" bug-fix-history subsection (§5 of the Task-Creation-Claim theme) — a query whose scoping had to be independently re-derived and widened multiple times — but for target *resolution* rather than execution-row *reset*; the two are siblings and probably belong in the same eventual consolidation.

### 6. Goto budget corrupted by a wrong-layer caller

**Problem.** `run_single_workflow`'s `max_iterations` parameter feeds `launch_params["max_iterations"]`, which service.py's own docstring maps directly to the engine's `max_total_gotos` override. It was only ever meant for `run_phase0`'s deliberate, narrow override (capping Phase 0's own decomposition-retry count at 3) — but `_run_one_feature` threaded the *same* value through to every regular feature pipeline launch too, sourced from the CLI's `--max-iterations` flag (default 3), which is semantically a **design-level** retry concept (a separate, correct mechanism, `MAX_DESIGN_RETRIES`, already exists for that) — a category error between two unrelated budgets that happened to share a parameter name. Every feature workflow in the system was silently capped at 3 total gotos across its entire 13-phase lifecycle instead of `workflow.yaml`'s real, deliberate `max_total_gotos: 30`. Live: `adversarial_review` found 3 real BLOCKERs and scored correctly (`score=0.4`, `action=goto`, `target=development`), but the engine forced `"continue"` instead — `"GOTO limit exceeded (6/3). Forcing continue to prevent infinite loop"` — silently waving real findings through to `security_review`. A related but separate budget-defaults bug: `feature_review`'s evaluation point had no `on_budget_exhausted` override, so the generic default (`"continue"`, a reasonable infinite-loop safety valve for most gates) applied even when the goto cap was hit on a *still-failing* review (BLOCKER/FIX findings, `score < 0.3`) — silently accepting a flawed decomposition as if it had passed instead of escalating to arbitration (`14926db`).

**Proper fix.** `run_single_workflow`'s `max_iterations` defaults to `None` (no override) instead of a hardcoded number, and is only written into `launch_params` when a caller explicitly passes one; `_run_one_feature` no longer forwards the CLI's value at all, letting `workflow.yaml`'s own `max_total_gotos` apply — `run_phase0`'s own explicit `max_iterations=3` call is untouched, since that one *is* the deliberate narrow override. `feature_review`'s evaluation point gets `on_budget_exhausted: arbitrate` explicitly, matching the existing precedent already used for `scope_review` in the main autopilot workflow, so exceeding the cap on a genuinely failing review escalates instead of silently passing.

**Representative commits:** `0352f90` (2026-08-13), `14926db` (2026-08-08).

**Relation to existing analysis.** New — the analysis doc doesn't currently discuss `max_iterations`/`--max-iterations` at all, or the `on_budget_exhausted` default's per-gate correctness implications; it documents `max_total_gotos`/`max_arbitrations_per_phase` as if they're the only inputs to the goto budget, when in practice a completely unrelated CLI flag intended for a different budget (`MAX_DESIGN_RETRIES`) silently overrode it for every feature workflow in the system until this fix landed one day before the theme's most recent commit — i.e., this was still an active, undiscovered bug for most of this document's own writing window.

---

**Overall assessment of this theme.** The characterization in the existing analysis doc — "one of the most patched-on-patch parts of the codebase" — holds up against the actual history, but the diffs reveal something the current-code read can't: this area didn't accumulate one kind of bug repeatedly, it accumulated **the same shape of bug at three different layers** — an in-memory counter that doesn't survive object recreation (§1), a persisted counter that doesn't reset on recovery (§2), and a query that doesn't scope to the current cycle (§5) — each rediscovered independently because the earlier fix didn't generalize to the next call site that needed the same discipline. The arbitration chain (§3) is the sharpest single finding: three sequential DB-constraint bugs meant the arbitration subsystem — the pipeline's designated escape hatch for exactly this class of stuck state — was itself completely non-functional in production, silently, for an unknown period, discovered only via a 30-hour live incident. Any refactor that consolidates the goto/retry/arbitration decision boundary should treat budget persistence, cycle-scoping, and arbitration-dispatch validity as three separate correctness properties to test explicitly, not one "make it work" pass — each has already been independently broken and independently fixed multiple times.

---

## Bug-Fix History: Status, Completion-Inference & Phase-Output-Routing Patterns

*Derived from a fresh read of the actual commit diffs behind 72 `fix:` commits in this theme, independent of the file-structure analysis above. ~35 `git show` calls, including several outside the given commit list (`bedfba2`, `2de15ad`, `1493c47`, `22178b1`) pulled in specifically to trace `status_derivation.py`'s caller history, per this pass's own request for a deeper spot-check than the file-structure pass gave it.*

#### Part A — Status & Completion-Inference Patterns

### A1. "All tasks done" trusted as "workflow/feature done," without checking that every phase actually ran — independently rediscovered at least four times, including inside the centralized module itself

**Problem.** The most common shape of bug in this theme: some code path derives "done" from `all(t.status == "done" for t in tasks)` and stops there. It's wrong whenever a workflow has phases that never got a task at all yet (a phase order gap, a phase whose task creation is still in flight, or — the live-observed case — a feature with 13 phases where only 2 ever had tasks). The fix is always the same shape (also join `PhaseExecution` and require every row `status == "completed"`), but it was written from scratch at least four separate times, spanning both the two-months-old code review and the centralized module the review told developers to use instead.

**Proper fix, in the order it was independently discovered:**
- **`e7d44ac`** (2026-08-03, `autopilot_api.py`'s `review_feature` approval handler): added `if all_tasks and all(t.status == "done" for t.status == "done") ... wf.status = "completed"` with no phase check at all.
- **`be5ff7b`** (2026-08-04, the very next day): found the same gap in *two* places at once — `_workflow_appears_abandoned` (`orchestrator.py`) and the exact `review_feature` block `e7d44ac` had added the day before. Both patched in the same commit to also join `PhaseExecution` and require `status != "completed"` count `== 0`.
- **`2de15ad`** (2026-08-10, `src/core/status_derivation.py` itself): `derive_feature_status`'s `task_statuses == {DONE}` branch had the identical bug — "tech-debt feature with 13 phases, only 2 had tasks, derived 'completed' while stuck at scope_review." This is the *centralized, single-source-of-truth module* rediscovering the same bug 5-6 weeks after `be5ff7b` fixed it in two other call sites, and without either of those fixes' authors apparently checking whether `status_derivation.py` had the same hole.
- **`af99e8d`** (2026-08-10, same day as `2de15ad`): a *second* facet of the identical bug — both `derive_feature_status` and `review_feature`'s approve handler excluded `"skipped"` `PhaseExecution` rows from the completion check, while workflow-level derivation already treats `skipped` as terminal — so a feature with any conditionally-skipped review phase could never settle on "completed," and the self-heal sweep kept flipping it back to "active" moments after approval.

**Still live today.** `review_feature`'s approve handler (`src/mcp/autopilot_api.py:4375-4397`, current code) still contains its own hand-rolled `all_tasks`/`PhaseExecution` check, calling neither `derive_workflow_status` nor `derive_feature_status`, despite `status_derivation.py` existing since 2026-07-01. Its own comment at line 4383-4387 explicitly says the `"skipped"`-exclusion choice has to match `derive_feature_status`'s "or this same feature will flap back to active on the next self-heal poll" — i.e. the endpoint's author knows this is a second, hand-synced copy of the centralized function's logic, and left it that way anyway. This is the single clearest piece of evidence in this pass that SOLID_OO_REVIEW §2.1's "4+ places" count is a floor, not a ceiling, and that the problem was not closed by the module's existence.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** confirms and sharpens 2.1. The file-structure pass could only see the current code's four-way split; the diffs show the actual *history* of that split — a fix landing in two call sites one day, the identical bug being rediscovered inside the canonical module itself six weeks later, and a still-current comment acknowledging the duplication as a known, accepted cost rather than a bug to close.

### A2. `Feature.status` desynced from `Workflow.status` through exit paths that don't run the normal write

**Problem.** `_run_one_feature`'s normal happy path sets `feat_record.status = "active"` on start and `"completed"` at the very end of a full pipeline run. Every other way a workflow can reach a terminal state — a backend restart re-entering the function early, a resume-from-pause continuing an in-flight workflow directly without a full design re-walk, a workflow reactivated via `/recover`, a project-level pause — bypasses that end-of-function write, leaving `Feature.status` stuck at whatever an earlier, now-superseded call last set it to.

**Proper fix, one exit path at a time:**
- **`e5aff55`** (2026-07-20): the existing-workflow fast path inside `_run_one_feature` (re-entered after a restart, `wf.status` already `"completed"`) returned `"completed"` without ever touching `feat_record.status`. Fixed by syncing the Feature row inline on that exact return, in addition to the normal end-of-function write.
- **`1788cd5`** (2026-07-20, same day): `e5aff55` only patches the case where `_run_one_feature` is called again for the *same* feature — a resumed backend that continues a workflow directly (not via a full design re-walk) never calls `_run_one_feature` for that feature again at all, so nothing is left to fix it. Adds `_sync_stale_feature_statuses`, a **table-wide** self-heal (not scoped to any one workflow, by design, since the whole point is to catch features no workflow-scoped loop will ever revisit) wired into the same background sweep that drives `_advance_phases`.
- **`4ca55b3`** (2026-08-03): `_resume_interrupted_workflows` flipped `wf.status` from `paused`/`failed` back to `active` but never touched the linked `Feature.status`, which stayed `paused` — because, per the commit message, `derive_feature_status` checks `feature.status` *first* and returns `paused` without ever looking at the workflow. Fixed by syncing `feature.status = "active"` in the same block.
- **`1493c47`** (2026-07-15): a mirror-image bug in the same family — `_run_one_feature` collapsed *every* non-`"completed"` workflow status into `Feature.status = "failed"`, including the ordinary `"paused"` a feature legitimately gets while waiting its turn in a later sequential execution group. Because `derive_design_status` treats any failed feature as design-failed, this permanently failed the whole design the moment a later group's features were created, even while an earlier group was still actively completing. Fixed by giving `"paused"` its own `FeatureStatus.PAUSED` branch instead of folding it into `"failed"`.
- **`22178b1`** (2026-08-14, latest in this cluster): `pause_project_workflows` (the Pause-button handler) never set `Feature.status = "paused"` at all — it only touched `Agent`/`Task` rows — so paused features kept showing "Active" in the UI. The fix's own comment (`orchestrator/engine_client.py:251-259`) explicitly notes it's mirroring "the per-feature pause endpoint," i.e. a *third* independent Feature-pausing write site, and cites `derive_feature_status`'s actual branch logic by name to justify why the direct write is shaped the way it is — aware of the centralized function's rules, but still not calling it.

**Relation to file-structure analysis: sharper than what's there.** SOLID_OO_REVIEW §2.2 names `_update_feature_status` vs. the inline `run_feature_pipelines` write as "two competing write paths," and records that `bedfba2` (2026-07-01, same day as the review) consolidated the second into a call to the first. What that consolidation did *not* do — confirmed by every commit in this list postdating it — is make `_update_feature_status` itself route through `derive_feature_status`. The result: the write paths keep multiplying (this list alone adds four more: the fast-path sync, the table-wide sweep, the recover-endpoint sync, the pause-button sync), each hand-deriving what the "right" status is at that call site, several with comments that explicitly reference `derive_feature_status`'s logic without calling it. Consolidating call *sites* (§2.2's fix) is a materially different — and much smaller — win than routing them through one *function* (§2.1's fix); this trace shows the codebase kept doing the former, not the latter, for over a month after the review shipped.

### A3. `_advance_phases`' periodic sweep: self-healing task/workflow state combinations that shouldn't exist, discovered one incident at a time

**Problem.** A cluster of six commits, mostly Jun 26 – Aug 11, each adding one more "if I see *this specific* impossible-looking state combination, repair it" branch to the sweep. None of them are the *same* bug twice, but they're the same *shape* of bug: some write site left two denormalized fields disagreeing (`Task.status="done"` with a non-null `failure_reason`; `Workflow.status="failed"` with tasks still `pending`/`in_progress`; a phase's `done_tasks > 0` but the phase still blocked by an unrelated `failed` task; a `PhaseExecution` that never flips out of `pending` despite its task being `done`), and the sweep is where each one eventually got noticed and patched.

**Representative fixes:**
- **`515a061`** (2026-06-26): `check_phase_completion`'s incomplete-task filter treated `"failed"` as blocking, so *any* phase with one failed task (even alongside all-done real work — e.g. a spurious task an agent created via MCP for the wrong phase, whose tmux session died on init) could never advance, and the `done_tasks == 0` stalled-phase handler didn't fire either because `done_tasks > 0`. Fixed by removing `"failed"` from the blocking-status set — it's terminal, not "in progress."
- **`937936b`** (2026-06-26, same day): two writers — task/execution creation and `_start_next_phase` — could still touch `phase_executions` rows *after* `_complete_workflow` had already run, because the monitor's own cycle guard checked `wf_db_status != "active"` at cycle *start*, not at the moment of the actual write, and a stale spec-gate re-evaluation on an already-completed gated phase could still be in flight. Both call sites gained a workflow-status recheck immediately before the write.
- **`916dfcb`** (2026-08-04): a task can end up `status="done"` with a non-null `failure_reason` (gate validation failing *after* the task itself completed) — an inconsistent combination that silently blocks phase advancement forever since nothing treats "done" as "still needs attention." Sweep resets it to `"failed"` so it re-enters proper gate evaluation.
- **`deb4eb2`** (2026-08-04, same session): the mirror case — a workflow already marked `"failed"` can still have tasks sitting `pending`/`in_progress`/`assigned` (orphaned by whatever set the workflow status without touching its tasks). Sweep marks them `"failed"` too, so nothing waits forever for an agent that's never coming.
- **`0ca070d`** (2026-06-27): four bugs from one forensics pass, three of them completion-inference — `is_design_fully_complete` (the second SOLID_OO_REVIEW §2.1-named reimplementation) blocked on *any* failed task even when a retry on the same phase later succeeded, blocked on its own `DIAGNOSTIC:`-prefixed monitor-created tasks as if they were real pending work, and hardcoded "10 phases" as the completion bar instead of querying the actual phase count. All three are the same underlying mistake — trusting a raw signal (task status, task count) without the context needed to interpret it correctly.
- **`7857ef4`** (2026-08-11): a Phase-0 workflow that completes *out-of-band* (a backend restart interrupting the process that was supposed to run the synchronous "workflow finished → create Feature rows" tail) is left genuinely `"completed"` with zero `Feature` rows and no recovery path short of a full rerun. Adds `finalize_phase0_workflow`, an idempotent path callable from any place a phase0 workflow is observed completing (the generic `_complete_workflow` hook, and the review-approve endpoint), with a TOCTOU guard on the underlying `_create_feature_records` insert so the two new call sites can't race and double-create.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the Phase Management section already documents the `_advance_phases`/`mark_phase_complete` split and several of the same self-heal call sites by name (`_release_pending_phases_with_done_tasks`, `_release_stale_task_creation_claims`), but frames them as static structure, not as an accreting incident log. Read as a sequence, `0ca070d → 515a061/937936b → deb4eb2/916dfcb → 7857ef4` is six weeks of "the sweep is where every new impossible state combination eventually gets a bespoke repair branch" — consistent with, but a sharper illustration of, the file-structure section's characterization of `_advance_phases` as "self-healing anything the synchronous path missed."

### A4. Status/queue-depth API endpoints reading a signal that disagreed with the UI reading a different one

**Problem.** `/api/autopilot/status` independently re-derives several numbers (`running`, `queue_depth`, `designs_failed`) from a mix of an in-memory `service_status` dict, a `state` file, and ad hoc DB queries — a fourth and fifth "is this thing done/running" implementation, this time at the read/API layer rather than the write layer.

**Representative fixes:**
- **`ce96c7b`** (2026-07-09): `running` was derived purely from `Workflow.status in ("active", "running")` — but an agent can still be actively working on a task in a `"failed"` workflow (pipeline stalled, agent orphaned but alive). Added a second check: any `Agent.status in ("working", "starting", "idle")` joined through `current_task_id` to a task in one of the project's workflows, regardless of workflow status.
- **`9aebb80`** (2026-07-07): `queue_depth` counted files on disk (`Path.glob`), while the dashboard's queue panel reads from the DB — the two numbers could disagree. Fixed to use a DB count (`AutopilotDesign` rows with `status="pending"`) whenever `project_id` is provided, falling back to the filesystem count only for the global (no-project) case.
- **`f0fad95`** (2026-07-10): a `NameError` — `designs_failed` was referenced before being locally defined, but only on the specific branch reached right after a pause/stop (`running=False`, no service error, no error-type last event). The frontend's status poll always passes `project_id` and hits this branch on literally every request in that state, so the endpoint 500'd continuously and "pause" looked broken even though the underlying `AutopilotService.stop()` had worked correctly.
- **`2037e52`** (2026-06-29): `get_project_design_status` derived overall status by aggregating `Workflow.status` across all matching workflows — which includes transient gotos/retries that later resolved, so a design mid-retry looked "failed" in the UI even though it would recover on its own. Fixed to prefer `AutopilotDesign.status` (written by `run_design_aggregate`/the continuous pipeline) when it's set to something other than `pending`/`unknown`, falling back to the workflow-status heuristic only when the design-level field hasn't been computed yet.
- **`b8aecf9`** (2026-07-09): a bug inside `status_derivation.py` itself, only five weeks after `derive_design_status` was written — `pick_next_design` sets a design's status to `"pending"` as an explicit "queued for retry" state, but `derive_design_status` had no branch honoring that and immediately re-derived it back to `"active"`, creating a live fight between the orchestrator's own write and the "centralized" module's read-and-overwrite. Fixed by adding an explicit early-return for `design.status == "pending"`. Notable because it's not a caller bypassing the centralized function — it's the centralized function itself getting the "which signal wins" question wrong, one week after the review that named it "the single source of truth."

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the API-surface sections don't dig into `get_pipeline_status`'s multi-source derivation logic in this kind of depth; this cluster is a small addition documenting that the "N-th independent implementation" pattern reaches the read/API layer too, not just the DB-write layer the rest of this theme covers.

### A5. Tracing `status_derivation.py`'s caller history — a deeper check on SOLID_OO_REVIEW §2.1

The file-structure pass's spot-check found exactly one caller (`src/mcp/autopilot_api.py`) and flagged `orchestrator.py`/`phase_manager.py` as never calling it. Walking the module's own git history (`git log -- src/core/status_derivation.py`, 22 commits) plus a `git log -S` search for the import string in the two files named as non-callers gives a more complete — and more specific — picture:

- **`8c28484`** (2026-07-01, 15:37): creates `status_derivation.py` as part of an architecture-review remediation. At this point, no caller outside `autopilot_api.py` exists.
- **`bedfba2`** (2026-07-01, 16:27 — **50 minutes later, same session**): this commit is the direct answer to the "how much wiring actually happened" question. Its message states the intent explicitly: *"Route orchestrator.py/phase_manager.py's design/feature status writes through the centralized status_derivation.py instead of independently reimplementing 'is this done?' in 4+ places... this was the root cause of a recurring bug class."* It does two concrete things:
  - **`phase_manager.py`** *did* get wired: `_populate_feature_folder` (`phase_manager.py:1898-1915`, still present in current code) used to unconditionally write `design.status = "completed"` the moment one feature's workflow finished — SOLID_OO_REVIEW §2.1's own "most severe instance" example. `bedfba2` replaced that write with a call to `derive_design_status(session, design.id, write_back=True)`, with a comment citing "H-3 / SOLID review 2.1" directly. **This fix has held**: it's still the live code path today.
  - **`orchestrator.py`** got a narrower fix: the two competing `Feature.status = "skipped"` writers (§2.2) were consolidated into one call to `_update_feature_status` — but `_update_feature_status` itself was **not** changed to call `derive_feature_status`; it remains a direct-write function, just with one fewer duplicate call site.
- **Confirmed still true today**: `is_design_fully_complete` (`orchestrator/queue.py:47`) and `run_design_aggregate` (`orchestrator/__init__.py:2536`) — the two other reimplementations §2.1 names — were not touched by `bedfba2` and still independently compute "done" today (task-status/agent/branch checks in the first; an `all_completed`/`any_failed`/`all_skipped` boolean chain in the second), confirmed by reading both functions' current bodies.
- **`orchestrator.py` never became a `derive_*` caller, but later commits made its direct writers `status_derivation`-*aware* without wiring them in.** `git log -S` for the three `derive_*` function names against `orchestrator.py` turns up only comments, in two later commits:
  - **`1493c47`** (2026-07-15): fixing the paused-folded-into-failed bug (A2 above), the added code comment explains the fix is necessary *because* `derive_design_status` treats any failed feature as design-failed — reasoning about the centralized function's behavior to justify a change to a completely separate, uncoordinated write site.
  - **`22178b1`** (2026-08-14): fixing the pause-button gap (A2 above), the comment again explains the fix by name-checking `derive_feature_status`'s "only PAUSED-preserving check is feature.status already being PAUSED" branch.

  In both cases, the fix is a new direct write to `Feature.status`, reasoned about by reading `status_derivation.py`'s source to make sure the direct write won't be immediately re-derived into something else — not a call into the module. This is a weaker form of "wiring" than §2.1's proposed fix: it keeps the split-brain write-path problem (§2.2) alive while merely keeping the two brains' *opinions* in sync by hand, one incident at a time, six weeks apart.

**Net assessment.** SOLID_OO_REVIEW §2.1's "only one caller" claim was accurate at the moment it was written and inaccurate 50 minutes later — `phase_manager.py` became, and has remained, a second real caller, for exactly the instance the review called "most severe." But the broader claim the review was making — that "is this done" logic is reimplemented rather than centralized — is still true of `orchestrator.py`'s two named functions today, and A1/A2 above show the *bug class* the review was trying to close (four-plus disagreeing definitions of "done") kept recurring for over a month afterward, including once inside `status_derivation.py` itself (`2de15ad`, six weeks after `bedfba2`). The one-directional-wiring fix the review recommended as "highest-leverage" was real but partial: one call site out of several, landed same-day for the worst offender, never extended to the rest.

---

#### Part B — Phase-Output-Routing Patterns

### B1. Wrong output-file path assumption: worktree root vs. `docs/<phase>/` subfolder convention, re-derived independently at every read site

**Problem.** Multiple, unrelated call sites each hardcode their own guess at where a phase's declared output file lives — `.hephaestus/<name>`, `docs/<name>`, or (the convention that eventually won) `docs/<phase_name>/<name>` — and each guess only gets corrected when that specific call site's incident surfaces. There is no single function that resolves "where does phase X's output actually live" that all of these call.

**Representative fixes:**
- **`685258c`** (2026-08-03): report-lookup endpoints only checked `.hephaestus/feature_report.html`; agents commonly write to `docs/` instead. Adds a `docs/` fallback at three separate call sites (`_find_archived_feature_report`'s equivalent inline check, `get_workflow_feature_report`, `get_feature_record_report`) — three call sites, three separately-added fallbacks, not a shared helper.
- **`8772527`** (2026-08-11, eight days later): the same three-plus call sites needed widening *again* — `feature_report.html` actually lands in a `docs/doc_review/` subfolder once `doc_review.yaml`'s output convention changed, so each site's candidate-path list grows by one more entry, independently, again. The commit also fixes a premature `return None` in `_find_archived_feature_report` that stopped scanning other gallery directories sharing the same `workflow_id` after the first one lacked a report — a second, unrelated bug found while re-touching the same function for the path fix.
- **`d06f4ce`** (2026-08-11): `adversarial_review.yaml`'s own prompt told the agent to read `review.md` — a path that has never existed; the real file is `.hephaestus/architectural_review/review.md`. Fixed by correcting all three in-prompt references. This is the *prompt-instruction* mirror of the same convention drift B1's other fixes chase in application code — the phase-output path convention isn't enforced by any schema, so both the code that reads outputs and the YAML that tells agents where to *write* them can each independently drift from the real convention.
- **`3ecf9ce`** (2026-07-24): `security_review.yaml`'s own declared `outputs:` (and its `done_definitions:`/prompt text) said flat `security_report.md`, while the actual convention other gated phases (`architectural_review`, `qa_validation`, `adversarial_review`, `product_validation`) already used was `docs/<phase_name>/<file>`. Fixed by updating the phase's own YAML declaration to match its siblings' convention, rather than fixing a reader to tolerate the mismatch.
- **`4e7dc85`** (2026-06-24, earliest in this cluster, and an ancestor of the `af99e8d` fix already documented above the Bug-Fix History sections): output validation checked only the *per-agent* worktree (`wt_{agent_id}`) for the declared artifact, when the actual write landed in the *shared* workflow worktree — a category of the same "wrong worktree/path assumption" bug that `af99e8d` (2026-08-10, already documented in this file's Task & Dispatch Services analysis) would later generalize into "prefer the currently-completing task's own agent's worktree over the workflow's earliest `AgentWorktree` row," for the review-mode-reentry case specifically.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** `af99e8d`'s worktree-recovery fix is already documented (above, in the Task & Dispatch Services excerpt). What this pass adds is the surrounding cluster: the *same* underlying problem — no single canonical "resolve this phase's output path" function — recurs as a pure path-list bug (B1's other four commits) independently of the worktree-selection bug `af99e8d` fixes, and the two `8772527`/`685258c` commits show it recurring *within the same endpoint family* eight days apart, exactly the "widened, then widened again" shape the GOTO-reset finding in the Task-Creation-Claim section documents for a completely different subsystem.

### B2. Reading a prior phase's output under a filename that never matched what that phase actually writes

**Problem.** A downstream phase's prompt (`additional_notes:` in its YAML) tells the agent to read a specific filename for context from an earlier phase — but that filename was never the one the earlier phase's own `outputs:` declaration specifies, so the read silently returns nothing every single run, with no error surfaced anywhere (a missing-file read in a prompt body just means the agent doesn't see that context, not a pipeline failure).

**Representative fixes:**
- **`0ac1540`** (2026-08-12): `qa_validation.yaml` and `security_review.yaml` both instructed their agents to read `review_report.md` for adversarial review's findings — a file that has never existed; `adversarial_review.yaml`'s own `outputs:` declares `adversarial.md`. Both were silently reading nothing, every run, since whenever those instructions were written. `forensics_analysis.yaml` had the identical wrong filename, and was separately missing `challenge.md` (`design_review`'s output, never added when that phase was inserted into the pipeline) — two independent drift bugs in the same file, discovered together.
- **`95102a6`** (2026-08-12, same session as `0ac1540`): `security_review`, `qa_validation`, and `product_validation` (phase IDs 8, 9, 10) all told their agents to read `docs.md` — but `doc_review` (phase ID 11) runs *after* all three, so the file can't exist yet at any of those points. Same failure signature as `0ac1540` (dead read, no error), different root cause (temporal/ordering mistake rather than a plain filename typo) — fixed by deleting the dead reads rather than trying to find a substitute, since no earlier phase produces an equivalent.
- **`dbe7639`** (2026-07-15): `workflow.yaml`'s `required_output` mapping (the hard-floor gate) only listed `qa_result.json`/`product_validation.json` for two gated phases, omitting their sibling `.md` report files — meaning a phase could satisfy the hard floor by writing the JSON alone, even though downstream consumers (and the human-facing report) expect both. Widened both entries to lists requiring both files.
- **`6539f2b`** (2026-06-27): `forensics_analysis`'s own read step globbed `src/autopilot/phase_*.py` for "the actual prompts given to each agent" — files deleted by an earlier YAML migration. The agent had been reading nothing for however long the migration had been live, silently. Fixed to copy `config/workflows/autopilot/*.yaml` files into the docs folder instead, and updated the phase's own instructions to describe the YAML shape rather than the deleted Python shape.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** not discussed in the file-structure sections at all — those sections analyze the YAML/config *loading* pipeline (three independent readers of the same phase files, per the Phase Management section), not the *content* of what one phase's prompt tells its agent to read from another phase's output. This is a genuinely separate defect family: no schema or test catches a phase's prompt text referencing a sibling phase's filename incorrectly, so these bugs are only found by an agent (or a human) noticing the referenced content never shows up — several of them, per their own commit messages, went unnoticed for the phase's entire lifetime in the pipeline before being caught in the same forensics-style audit session (`0ac1540`/`95102a6`, same day).

### B3. Frontmatter `type:` field as a second, less visible output-routing mechanism — mismatched between prompt instructions and the parser, and briefly flip-flopped mid-fix

**Problem.** Gated phases (`architectural_review`, `adversarial_review`, `qa_validation`, `product_validation`, `scope_review`) don't just write output to a *path* — they also embed a YAML frontmatter block with a `type:` field inside that file, which `src/autopilot/spec.py`'s `validate_gate_result_schema` checks against `expected_type = phase_name` (`spec.py:1370`, no suffix) to confirm the file is "the one documented in `<phase>.yaml`, not an incompatible/missing block." Getting this field's exact expected string right in the prompt instructions is its own routing problem, parallel to B1/B2's file-path problem but operating on file *content* instead of file *location*.

**Fix sequence — all four commits landed within 15 minutes of each other on 2026-08-04, and the middle two directly reverse each other:**
- **`66401a1`** (04:16:22): adds a warning to all gated-phase YAMLs insisting the type must be exactly `'<phase>_result'` (e.g. `architectural_review_result`) — "not `'architectural_review'`."
- **`b648e90`** (04:18:55, **2.5 minutes later**): reverses `66401a1` — changes every gated-phase YAML's documented `type:` value to the bare phase name, *removing* the `_result` suffix `66401a1` had just insisted on. Cross-checking against `spec.py:1370`'s actual runtime check (`expected_type = phase_name`, confirmed by reading the current source) shows `b648e90` is the version that matches what the code actually validates — `66401a1` had briefly reinforced the wrong convention before being caught and corrected in the same session.
- **`737edb8`** (04:27:53) and **`4129af2`** (04:30:58): a related but distinct bug in the same cluster — prompts told agents to write the report body *first*, then "prepend" the frontmatter block as a separate step, which in practice meant the frontmatter sometimes never got added (agent calls the file done after step N, before reaching the "now prepend" step). Reworded every gated-phase YAML to instruct writing the frontmatter *as the first bytes of a single write*, with an explicit `⚠️ DO NOT WRITE THE REPORT FIRST ⚠️` warning.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** not discussed — the file-structure sections cover `spec.py`'s `score_*` functions and `GATE_RESULT_ARTIFACTS`/`GATE_RESULT_SUBDIR` as static structure, not the fact that the frontmatter contract briefly disagreed with itself mid-session, or that the "write frontmatter last" ordering bug was its own separate, independently-discovered failure mode from the "wrong type string" one. Worth flagging on its own: this is a case where the *fix itself* went the wrong direction first (`66401a1`) before the ~2.5-minutes-later correction (`b648e90`) — a small but concrete illustration of how easy it is to get this convention wrong even while actively trying to fix it, since nothing enforces the `type:` string against the parser except an agent (or engineer) manually cross-checking `spec.py`.

### B4. Synthetic/forced-completion artifacts written in the wrong schema — status-inference and output-routing bugs compounding in one function

**Problem.** `_cap_out_review_phase` is a *status*-forcing mechanism (it stops a review phase from re-running past `max_review_runs` by synthesizing a "clean pass" result and force-completing the phase) that also has to write a real *output* file, because `mark_phase_complete`'s downstream scorer reads it back from disk on the next evaluation. Getting the file's schema wrong is simultaneously a completion-inference bug (the phase "passes" a review it never actually ran) and an output-routing bug (the artifact written doesn't match what that phase's own scorer expects to parse).

**The bug and fix — `99d19b6`** (2026-07-23): `_cap_out_review_phase` wrote one hardcoded shape, `{"blocker_count": 0, "capped": True, ...}`, for *every* gated phase it capped. `score_architectural_review`/`score_adversarial_review` do read `blocker_count` — but `score_qa` reads `pass_rate`/`failed_tests`/`requirements_met_rate`, `score_product_validation` reads `verdict`/`unmet_requirements`, and `score_scope_review` reads `verdict`. Handed a `blocker_count`-only file, `score_qa` computed `total_tests=0 → pass_rate=0%` — the worst possible score, the exact opposite of the "clean pass" this code path exists to produce. Observed live: `qa_validation` hit its review-run cap, the cap-out wrote the wrong shape, scored 0%, and immediately `goto`'d back to `development` — burning through `max_total_gotos` *faster* than not capping at all would have, in a sub-minute loop. Fixed by adding `synthetic_clean_result(phase_name, run_count)`, a per-phase-schema builder that returns the shape each specific scorer actually reads.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** new — the file-structure pass's Phase Management section documents `_cap_out_review_phase`'s status-forcing role (bypassing `evaluate()` to avoid its own retry-count check racing the synthetic "clean" score) but doesn't discuss what it writes to disk or that the write was wrong for three of five gated phases. Worth naming as its own instance of "N-th independent implementation" — five separate `score_*` functions, each with its own schema, and one write site that had to know all five but only got one of them right until this fix.

### B5. Gate-scorer schema brittleness: `score_*` functions silently misreading agent-written JSON

**Problem.** Distinct from B4 (a code path writing the wrong shape), this cluster is agents themselves writing a JSON shape that's reasonable-looking but doesn't match what the corresponding `score_*` function in `spec.py` expects — nested instead of flat, missing entirely when only the `.md` companion was written, or a verdict string the scorer's exact-match logic doesn't recognize.

**Representative fixes:**
- **`3ddc622`** (2026-06-27): agents sometimes wrote `{"scope_review": {"verdict": "PASS"}, ...}` (nested) instead of the documented flat `{"verdict": "PASS", ...}`. `score_scope_review` now normalizes both forms, plus a third fallback (`analysis_summary.scope_drift_detected`), and the YAML gained an explicit "WRONG — do NOT write this" counter-example.
- **`6deb468`** (2026-07-21): when an agent wrote `product_validation.md` but not the companion `.json`, `score_product_validation` unconditionally scored it `_DEV` (send back to development), forever, regardless of what the prose report actually said — because nothing read the `.md` as a fallback. Fixed to extract a PASS verdict from the report text when the JSON is missing, matching a pattern `adversarial_review`/`architectural_review`'s scorers already used.
- **`78bc52e`** (2026-07-21, same day): `score_product_validation` also overrode an agent's explicit `PASS_WITH_MINOR_GAPS` verdict with `_DEV` purely because `unmet_requirements` was non-empty — even though the agent had *already judged* those specific gaps non-blocking. Fixed by checking for that verdict string before the hard-floor rejection.
- **`21fa097`** (2026-08-01): `score_scope_review` required *both* `out_of_scope` and `missing` to be empty for a PASS — a mechanical list-emptiness rule that directly contradicted `scope_review.yaml`'s own prompt text, which told the agent to list benign items transparently while still passing. An agent that correctly judged an item benign and wrote `verdict=PASS` still scored `0.2` (FAIL) because its lists were non-empty, sending the pipeline back to `product_requirements`, which regenerated the same reasonable content, `scope_review` re-approved it, the scorer re-failed it again — 162 gotos and all 3 arbitration attempts burned on this exact unwinnable loop before the workflow gave up, killing an in-flight development task as collateral damage. Fixed by trusting the agent's `verdict` field directly instead of re-deriving it from list contents.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the Phase Management section documents that gate scoring "is entirely separate from `WorkflowOrchestrator`'s condition evaluator" and that the YAML's `evaluator: heuristic` field is misleading for a gated phase — but doesn't get into the individual `score_*` functions' schema-tolerance bugs. This cluster is the concrete cost of that architecture: five hand-written parsers, each independently prone to being stricter than the prompt that's supposed to produce their input, with a documented 162-goto runaway loop as the most severe instance.

### B6. Review-mode / `request_changes` re-entry keeping `Feature.status`/`Workflow.status` correctly parked mid-fix

**Problem.** When a human requests changes on a feature under human review, the feature needs to stay visibly "in review" (paused, yellow border) while a corrective development task runs — but the code that creates that corrective task shares a lot of machinery with the *normal* resume-from-pause path, which actively wants to clear the pause. Getting the two paths' side effects to not step on each other is its own small instance of "which write site owns this status field right now."

**Fixes:**
- **`630f642`** (2026-08-03): `review_feature`'s `request_changes` branch was reusing the same "resume the workflow" logic as plain approval — clearing `wf.status`/`wf.paused_by` and setting `feature.status = "active"` immediately, which made the feature look approved and unpaused the moment a corrective task was merely *queued*, before any fix had actually landed. Fixed by leaving `wf.status`/`wf.paused_by`/`feature.status` untouched (still `"paused"`/`"review"`) when creating the feedback task — the feature only leaves review mode once a human explicitly approves afterward.
- **`34e6a92`** (2026-08-03, same evening): a `db.flush()` gap in the same code path — the new corrective task's `id` wasn't populated in memory until commit, so building the `to_restart` list (used right after to spawn agents) could read a `None` id. Added the flush plus logging at each step (task created, no `architectural_review` phase found, agent spawn triggered) specifically so a repeat of the silent-no-op failure this whole area had could be diagnosed from logs instead of re-discovered live.

**Relation to file-structure analysis:** the Workflow Termination & API Surface section's review-mode discussion doesn't dig into this specific request-changes vs. approve status-ownership conflict; worth naming as one more small instance of the same theme as A2/A6 — a status field with more than one plausible "owner" at a given moment, this time resolved correctly (keep it parked) rather than by adding another self-heal sweep.

---

**Overall assessment of this theme.** The status/completion-inference half (Part A) is the single strongest evidence in any of this document's bug-fix-history passes that centralizing "is this done" logic into `status_derivation.py` did not, by itself, close the bug class it was built to close. The wiring trace (A5) shows the fix that *did* land (`bedfba2`, `phase_manager.py`) happened within the hour, for exactly the worst-named instance — but every other independent implementation named by SOLID_OO_REVIEW §2.1 (`is_design_fully_complete`, `run_design_aggregate`, and now several more found in this pass: `review_feature`'s approve handler, `_workflow_appears_abandoned`, even `status_derivation.py`'s own `derive_feature_status` at one point) kept the same underlying "all tasks done ≠ all phases done" bug alive independently for weeks to months afterward, with `review_feature`'s copy still live and un-migrated in the current codebase.
